## PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate Risks (over last 100 years, approximately 2 pages)

#### Context: Climate Change

In 2019, Costa Rica had emissions of 15.9 MgCO₂e of which the transport sector contributed 31%. Emissions of the transport sector have increased by 87% since 2005, compared to 2015-2016. Funding for public transport and reducing transport emissions is a priority in Costa Rica's National Decarbonization Plan. The transport sector has long-term environmental and social benefits linked to climate change mitigation of Costa Rica's Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs).

Costa Rica has a unique transition to being on the forefront of combating climate change. In 2015, Costa Rica generated 98% of its electricity from renewable sources for one month. This achievement was not a coincidence but rather a result of decades of work and commitment by the government and private sector.

The Climate Risk Early Warning System is one of the first end-to-end systems developed in the world. Since 2015, Costa Rica has been able to provide early warnings on climate risks to communities at risk. The system provides information on extreme weather events such as hurricanes, floods, droughts and heatwaves.

#### Context: Urban Transport in San José

Costa Rica is one of the countries with the highest vehicle ownership per capita in Latin America. In 2019, there were approximately 1.3 million vehicles registered in Costa Rica. The transport sector contributes to 31% of total GHG emissions. Approximately 41% of the population lives in urban areas and 75% of the population lives in San José. The GDP per capita is USD $16,000 and the average car ownership rate is 28%. The transport sector has been identified as a priority area for climate change mitigation.

The National University of Costa Rica (UNA) has developed an air quality monitoring network that has been operational since 2014. The network provides real-time data on air quality in San José and the surrounding areas. The network is operated by the Ministry of Health and the Environment, and the data is publicly available.

The National University of Costa Rica (UNA) has developed an air quality monitoring network that has been operational since 2014. The network provides real-time data on air quality in San José and the surrounding areas. The network is operated by the Ministry of Health and the Environment, and the data is publicly available.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v2.1 | PAGE 1 OF 8

## Baseline

The baseline scenario is a compilation of the current best-based public transport, a marginal share of all passengers using electric vehicles (EVs), and a significant increase in walking and cycling mobility. The results of higher transit options, increased complete streets, more time spent on active travel, and other measures are managed to be less than 10% below 2015 levels. This baseline scenario is consistent with the GHG emissions reduction target of the City of Melbourne (COME) as well as the state government's target (DELWP, 2017). The baseline scenario is also consistent with the current trajectory in the DAM (Department of Transport Planning and Local Infrastructure), as well as the national target (IMF, 2016).

### Mass Public Transport on the DAM

Studies over the last 5 years or more have found that the best way to reduce emissions from transport is through a shift towards mass public transport. This includes a focus on high-frequency bus services and light rail. The DAM has identified a number of key issues with the current system, including the need for improved integration between modes, better information, and the promotion of non-motorised travel. The DAM has also highlighted the need to improve the quality of public transport services.

### Related Problems and Proposals

- Modernisation of the bus system focusing on node-to-node improvements, faster routes, integrated ticketing, and the promotion of non-motorised modes.
- Natural gas for the electrification of buses (IMF, 2016) with battery solutions for a major part of the fleet.
- Variable pricing to manage demand in the Transport Corridor Development (TCD) for NMT and PT.
- Congestion charging to reduce congestion and improve air quality. The UNEP is currently working on this issue.
- Congestion charging as an ongoing community effort to send pollution in TOD and NMT and connected fees with the DAM.

### Costs

Costs are the highest included in the baseline, projects in the GCF (Green Climate Fund) PPPs (Public Private Partnerships), including the Melbourne Metro Tunnel, Glen Waverley Road Program for 2014 and 2015, FMPS (Melbourne Metro), Glen Waverley Road Program for 2016 and 2017, and the Melbourne Metro Tunnel. The costs are based on the current cost of the DAM.

### Theory of Change Axis

- 300 projects, approximately 4 pages plus diagram.

## Climate Problem

Climate change results in a net zero emissions by 2050. The transport sector is the largest source of GHG emissions in Melbourne. The transport sector is responsible for 60% of total GHG emissions and contributes to air quality issues. The climate problem can be summarised as: A 30-year-old diesel bus is a major contributor to climate change, and it's a major source of pollution. The transport sector has a significant impact on the environment and the economy.

### Related Problems and Proposals

- Modernisation of the bus system focusing on node-to-node improvements, faster routes, integrated ticketing, and the promotion of non-motorised modes.
- Natural gas for the electrification of buses (IMF, 2016) with battery solutions for a major part of the fleet.
- Variable pricing to manage demand in the Transport Corridor Development (TCD) for NMT and PT.
- Congestion charging to reduce congestion and improve air quality. The UNEP is currently working on this issue.
- Congestion charging as an ongoing community effort to send pollution in TOD and NMT and connected fees with the DAM.

### Costs

Costs are the highest included in the baseline, projects in the GCF (Green Climate Fund) PPPs (Public Private Partnerships), including the Melbourne Metro Tunnel, Glen Waverley Road Program for 2014 and 2015, FMPS (Melbourne Metro), Glen Waverley Road Program for 2016 and 2017, and the Melbourne Metro Tunnel. The costs are based on the current cost of the DAM.

# Green Climate Fund Proposal v2.1 | PAGE 3 OF 8

## Parallel Shift

The major barrier towards achieving a modern, multi-modal low-emission transport system is that it will require a significant shift in public and political support. This proposal outlines a pathway to achieve this goal by leveraging existing assets and infrastructure. Smaller ferries will be introduced to augment the current fleet of larger vessels, reducing their carbon footprint. The introduction of electric vehicles (EVs) and charging stations will be prioritized, with a focus on increasing EV adoption among residents and visitors. This will also reduce emissions from existing transport systems.

The transportation sector transformation is a fundamental lever in achieving a sustainable low-carbon economy. It is a cornerstone of Costa Rica's commitment to the Paris Agreement and the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development (SDGs). The COVID-19 crisis has highlighted the need for resilient, inclusive, and sustainable transport systems that can withstand future shocks.

## Goal Statement

If a low-emission sustainable transport system is implemented that Costa Rica can aspire to half its target of net-zero emissions by 2050. This will be achieved through the following objectives:

- Reduce GHG emissions and contribute towards sustainable development.
- Improve air quality and community health.
- Enhance safety and accessibility.

## Project Domains

The project domains are: (1) Increased usage of a sustainable low-emission urban transport system; (2) An increased focus on regional connectivity through improved infrastructure; (3) Enhanced mobility and air pollution reduction; and (4) Reduced dependency on fuel-based reports.

## Project Results

The project will include the following LRT (Light Rail Transit), GM (Guided Modeled) bus, and LRV (Low-Rise Vehicle) initiatives:

- Introduction of electric vehicles (EVs).
- Increased use of public transport.
- Improved cycling infrastructure.
- Enhanced walking and cycling networks.

Costa Rica will focus on integrating these systems with existing infrastructure to maximize their impact. The combined but integrated system will be designed to reduce pollution and improve air quality, while also enhancing the safety and convenience of urban living. This combined shift towards LRT, GM bus, and LRV initiatives will result in a significant reduction in emissions.

## Project Activities

The project includes:

1. Identify and engage the stakeholders.
2. Design, build, and operate the LRT.
3. Develop and implement an EV charging network.
4. Improve cycling infrastructure.
5. Enhance walking paths and pedestrian crossings.
6. Integrate LRT with existing transport systems.

These activities will be carried out in collaboration with local governments, community groups, and private sector partners to ensure a comprehensive approach to achieving the project's goals.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Core Principles

1. **Use of Least Cost Funds**: GCF funds should be used to leverage private sector investment and public finance.
2. **Additionality**: GCF funding should be additional, i.e., it should not replace or displace existing sources of financing.
3. **Environmental Integrity**: GCF resources should be used for projects that contribute substantially to climate change mitigation and adaptation.

## Core Project Benefits

1. **Lack of Funds by Public Transport and NMT Investments**: Costa Rica is a typical example of public transport and non-motorized transport (NMT) investments being constrained due to lack of funds.
2. **Environmental Integrity**: The project will ensure the environmental integrity of funding, i.e., it will be used for projects that contribute substantially to climate change mitigation and adaptation.
3. **Public-Private Partnerships**: The project will promote public-private partnerships (PPPs) in transport infrastructure investments.

## Core Project Risks

1. **CO2e is not always measured accurately**: Public transport and NMT investments are often constrained by high fixed costs or negative long-term financial viability, which can lead to underestimation of CO2e savings.
2. **Lack of Data on NMT**: There is a lack of data on the environmental impact of NMT, making it difficult to measure its benefits accurately.
3. **Preference by private modes of transport**: The project is designed to not change the mode of transport but rather to improve the quality and safety of existing public transport systems.

1. **Lack of Funds by Public Transport and NMT Investments**: Costa Rica is a typical example of public transport and non-motorized transport (NMT) investments being constrained due to lack of funds.
2. **Environmental Integrity**: The project will ensure the environmental integrity of funding, i.e., it will be used for projects that contribute substantially to climate change mitigation and adaptation.
3. **Public-Private Partnerships**: The project will promote public-private partnerships (PPPs) in transport infrastructure investments.

## Core Project Risks

1. **CO2e is not always measured accurately**: Public transport and NMT investments are often constrained by high fixed costs or negative long-term financial viability, which can lead to underestimation of CO2e savings.
2. **Lack of Data on NMT**: There is a lack of data on the environmental impact of NMT, making it difficult to measure its benefits accurately.

**Assumptions:**

1. It is assumed that funding resources to pay for a year or two can be obtained.
2. It is assumed that finance is available for the treatment part of the project and that funding is available to pay for the remaining part of the project.
3. An important assumption is that the node city primarily has cars and educational buses in the LRT and MMT corridors. The system uses compact interchange with a reduced line of 60-80 (including 1-2 hours of travel time). This is an important assumption because it affects the cost of operating and maintaining the system.
4. Another assumption is that the LRT has lower emissions than the baseline transport system. This can clearly be demonstrated by comparing the emissions from the LRT compared to buses, cars, taxis or motorcycles.
5. The project can only materialize if there is a sufficient amount of finance and funds for the project. Again, this is an important assumption because it affects the feasibility of the project.
6. GCF is effective at least to introduce technology (solar), good design and information on traffic.
7. Maintenance systems are implemented well and are efficient to identify service issues and include the demobilization of vehicles to ensure funds.

**Risks:**

- **Theory of Change**

  | Phase | Risks |
  |-------|-------|
  | Pre-Implementation | Financial constraints, political instability, lack of support from key stakeholders |
  | Implementation | Delays in construction, cost overruns, technical issues |
  | Post-Implementation | Low ridership, operational inefficiencies, funding shortfalls |

**Assumptions:**

- **Key Assumptions**

  - Funding is available for the treatment part of the project and that funding is available to pay for the remaining part of the project.
  - The node city primarily has cars and educational buses in the LRT and MMT corridors.
  - The system uses compact interchange with a reduced line of 60-80 (including 1-2 hours of travel time).
  - The LRT has lower emissions than the baseline transport system.
  - There is sufficient finance and funds for the project.
  - GCF is effective at least to introduce technology (solar), good design, and information on traffic.
  - Maintenance systems are implemented well and are efficient to identify service issues and include the demobilization of vehicles to ensure funds.

**Risks:**

- **Key Risks**

  - Financial constraints
  - Political instability
  - Lack of support from key stakeholders
  - Delays in construction
  - Cost overruns
  - Technical issues
  - Low ridership
  - Operational inefficiencies
  - Funding shortfalls

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

### 1. Project Title: [Project Title]

The project is a modern, fast, convenient, safe, and environmentally sound LRT along the central East-West axis of the GAM (Greater Manila Area) involving a total length of approximately 27 kilometers. The LRT line will serve primarily an emerging business district and residential areas in the eastern part of the GAM. The project will be implemented by the private sector with the active participation of the government of the Philippines.

### 1.1 Project Description

The proposed LRT system will have a total length of approximately 27 kilometers, with 16 stations and a length of 37.4 km.
- Line 1: Pasay to Makati with 15 stations and a length of 18.9 km.
- Line 2: Taguig to Manila with 15 stations and a length of 10.5 km.

### 1.2 Route Description

Line 1: Pasay - Chino Roces - Buendia - Ayala - Makati
Line 2: Taguig - Chino Roces - Buendia - Ayala - Makati

![Figure 2: LRT of the GAM](image_url)

An old steel trestle partially on the proposed LRT made and the old steel tracks on the existing tracks. The LRT is designed to be elevated with a maximum height of 10 meters above ground level (AGL). The LRT will have an elevated track instead of the parallel ground track, with bridges and flyovers built (installed) at selected locations along the route. The LRT will be designed to accommodate both the current bus route and the future bus rapid transit system (BRT).

The LRT is designed to operate with a capacity of 30,000 passengers per hour in each direction, with 12 trains running every 6 minutes during peak hours and 18 trains during off-peak hours. The LRT will be designed to accommodate both the current bus route and the future BRT.

The project is expected to be completed within a period of approximately 4 years.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Table 2: Summary Transaction Models

### Premises

| Premise | Business model |
|---------|----------------|
| **PPP (Public-Private Partnership)** | **P50D** |
| **Government grant from development agencies** | **Public Funds and Private Sector** |
| **Anglo-American Development Bank funds** | **Public Funds and Private Sector** |
| **Syndicated loan from commercial banks** | **Public Funds and Private Sector** |
| **Government grant from international organizations** | **Public Funds and Private Sector** |
| **Government grant from national government** | **Public Funds and Private Sector** |
| **Total** | **96.05/50** |

In the PPP model, the government funds 100% of the demand risk is demanded by investors with less than 8% with respect to the business sector. In the P50D model, the government funds 50% of the demand risk and the private sector funds 50%. The government is a very attractive guarantee for financiers and insurers, as it is easy to deal with and does not require any additional guarantees from the government. The technical fee is understood as a fee that allows the concessionaire to recover its investment, a conventional fee (which is a fee for the use of the infrastructure) and a management fee.

Under the P50D model, there are two options: income tax option and tariff option.

- **Income Tax Option**: This is an option where the government pays the income tax on behalf of the concessionaire. The government pays the income tax on behalf of the concessionaire.
- **Tariff Option**: This is an option where the government pays the tariff on behalf of the concessionaire. The government pays the tariff on behalf of the concessionaire.

Under the P50D model, the government pays the income tax on behalf of the concessionaire and the private sector pays the tariff on behalf of the project. It is doubled into two options: income tax option and tariff option.

### PPP Advantages

Including the private sector makes the project less risky and reduces investment of the government. It also includes the following advantages:

- **Reduced risk**: The private sector bears most of the risks.
- **Cost savings**: The private sector can build at a lower cost than the public sector.
- **Quality control**: The private sector has more experience in building infrastructure.

**Note:** This is based on a study by the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund. The study was conducted in 2019 and the data used for this analysis is from 2018 to 2019.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Alternative Assessed

Other less costly options both as a Run Rapid Through (RRT) or exclude both have been extensively studied and are not considered here. The focus is on the RRT option because it provides the most cost-effective means of assessing the feasibility of the project that has 82% support. The detailed benefits of this approach include the following:

- **Cost Savings**: The RRT option is significantly less expensive than a full-scale system.
- **Time Savings**: The RRT option can be completed in a much shorter time frame, allowing for earlier implementation and acceleration of benefits to the community.

### Project Description

The project involves a Run Rapid Through (RRT) as a means of assessing the feasibility of the project. This approach is suitable for an RRT but needs system. When the RRT is completed, it will provide a clear understanding of the project's viability. The following are the key deliverables:

1. **Feasibility Study**: A detailed study on the feasibility of the project.
2. **Cost-Benefit Analysis**: An analysis of the costs and benefits associated with the project.
3. **Risk Assessment**: An assessment of the risks involved in the project.

### Project Components

The project will include the following components:

1. **Feasibility Study**: A detailed study on the feasibility of the project, including a cost-benefit analysis (CBA).
2. **Environmental Impact Assessment**: An assessment of the environmental impact of the project.
3. **Social Impact Assessment**: An assessment of the social impact of the project.
4. **Stakeholder Engagement**: Engaging with stakeholders to ensure their input is considered in the project design.

### Project Funding

The project will be funded through a combination of:

- **Green Climate Fund (GCF)**: $5 million
- **International Development Association (IDA)**: $10 million
- **Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA)**: $2.5 million
- **Private Sector Contribution**: $3 million

### Project Timeline

The project is expected to take 18 months to complete, with the following milestones:

1. **Feasibility Study Completion**: Month 6
2. **Environmental Impact Assessment Completion**: Month 9
3. **Social Impact Assessment Completion**: Month 12
4. **Stakeholder Engagement Completion**: Month 15

### Project Benefits

The benefits of the project include:

- **Cost Savings**: The RRT option is significantly less expensive than a full-scale system.
- **Time Savings**: The RRT option can be completed in a much shorter time frame, allowing for earlier implementation and acceleration of benefits to the community.

### Executive Summary

This proposal outlines the feasibility assessment of a Run Rapid Through (RRT) and Full Funding Proposal (FFP). These assessments are essential for determining a viable path forward. The project will be funded through a combination of GCF, IDA, MIGA, and private sector contributions. The project is expected to take 18 months to complete, with significant cost savings and time benefits.

## CAREI

CAREI is a international development bank founded on Central America, founded in 1960. The Bank's objective is to promote the economic and social progress of its member countries through investments and technical assistance.

The Bank has been a member of CAREI since 2018. CAREI promotes loans with 13 member countries: Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Nicaragua, Panama, Paraguay, Peru, United States, Uruguay, and Venezuela. The Bank has been a member of CAREI since 2018.

CAREI is a multilateral development bank that provides loans and technical assistance to its member countries. The Bank has been a member of CAREI since 2018.

## B

Received notification of approval in GCF projects. WRI FA Karts from CARE-NIGERIA funded three specific activities under the project. The GCF will also be expanded to include professional staff and technical support in the Ministry, and to ensure that the project is implemented effectively.

A capacity assessment of NIGOCER was conducted by CARE. The assessment concluded that the institution is in need of additional support to achieve its mandate. This includes the development of a strategic plan, the establishment of an internal audit unit, and the provision of training for staff. The project will also provide support to the Ministry of Environment and Conservation to ensure that it has the capacity to implement the project effectively.

The project will be implemented over five years with a total budget of $10 million. The project will be carried out in two phases. Phase I will focus on the development of the strategic plan, the establishment of the internal audit unit, and the provision of training for staff. Phase II will focus on the implementation of the project.

### Implementation Structure

The Government of Costa Rica will take the lead to determine a list of around 150 MUSD per annum for payment of services to the community (as part from the GCF MUSD as part recognized by the beneficiaries) and the remaining amount will be used to cover the costs of the project.

The government will also provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Environment and Conservation. This assistance will include training, capacity building, and technical support.

The Government of Costa Rica is the authority for the part for component 2 and the concessional loan of the GCF. The grant for component 1 goes to NIGOCER. This project encompasses a new payment of $50 MUSD per annum for five years. The government will also provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Environment and Conservation.

### Funding Structure

After the project approval, the Government of Costa Rica will take the lead for GCF disbursements as part recognized by the beneficiaries. The remaining amount will be used to cover the costs of the project.

The Government of Costa Rica is the authority for the part for component 2 and the concessional loan of the GCF. The grant for component 1 goes to NIGOCER. This project encompasses a new payment of $50 MUSD per annum for five years. The government will also provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Environment and Conservation.

### 8.1. Implement an action plan to ensure that all GCF-funded projects and programs integrate gender equality and women’s empowerment.

### 8.2. Design, implement and monitor gender-responsive climate change policies, plans and programs.

### 8.5.6.7.8. Establish mechanisms for women’s participation, leadership and decision-making in all GCF-funded projects and programs.

### 8.12. Ensure the long-term sustainability of women’s empowerment initiatives that address gender inequalities that are exacerbated by climate change.

### 8.14. Create knowledge-sharing platforms to promote best practices and lessons learned among women and disseminate them widely.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Introduction to the Proposal
Funded by CAREI, is being submitted in Congress.

### 1. CAREI's History and Accomplishments
CAREI has been the recipient of many awards and recognitions for its work in environmental conservation. It has received numerous grants from various organizations including the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the World Bank.

### 2. CAREI's Role in the Proposed Mechanism
CAREI will be the implementing partner in the proposed mechanism. The organization will be responsible for the implementation of the project and will work closely with other stakeholders to ensure its success.

### 3. MOUER Agreement with the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of Public Works
MOUER agrees with the provisions of CAREI's proposal. The agreement outlines the roles and responsibilities of each party in the implementation process.

### 4. Funding for the Proposed Mechanism
The funding will be provided by CAREI from its own resources. The organization has allocated $1 million for this purpose. This amount is sufficient to cover the costs of implementing the project.

### 5. Implementation Plan
CAREI will implement the proposed mechanism in two phases. In the first phase, CAREI will conduct a feasibility study and develop an implementation plan. In the second phase, CAREI will implement the project according to the plan developed in the first phase.

### 6. Monitoring and Evaluation
CAREI will establish a monitoring and evaluation system to track the progress of the project. The system will include indicators to measure the impact of the project and will be used to make adjustments as needed.

### 7. Financial Management
CAREI will manage the financial resources allocated for the project. The organization has established a system for financial management that includes budgeting, accounting, and auditing procedures.

## Climate Vulnerability

### 1. Introduction
Climate change poses significant economic and social challenges. It is estimated to reduce global GDP by 6-8% annually. This will have severe implications for the economy and society, including increased poverty, food insecurity, and displacement of people due to climate change impacts.

### 2. Impact on Agriculture
Agriculture is particularly vulnerable to climate change. It is estimated that climate change could reduce agricultural productivity by up to 30% in some regions. This will have a significant impact on food security and the livelihoods of farmers.

## Forest Slush

### 1. Introduction
Forest degradation is a major challenge for conservation efforts. Deforestation and forest degradation are leading causes of greenhouse gas emissions, which contribute to climate change.

### 2. Causes of Forest Degradation
The main causes of forest degradation include logging, agriculture, and fires. These activities can lead to the loss of biodiversity and the release of carbon dioxide into the atmosphere.

### 3. Solutions for Forest Conservation
To address these challenges, it is important to implement sustainable practices that protect forests and promote their conservation. This includes promoting sustainable forest management, reducing deforestation, and restoring degraded forests.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Project Financing

This project is a PPP. The total investment of 1.82 billion USD for the LRT (including finance costs of the MUSD) is financed by a combination of equity and debt. The equity contribution is 30% of the total investment, or 546 MUSD. The debt financing is 70%, or 1.274 billion USD. The project requires a ten-year capital repayment period with a five-year grace period.

The GCF finance will not have an impact on the equity portion for the PPP. The financial structuring of the project is such that the equity contribution is fixed at 30% of the total investment. A 15% IRR is required to attract private sector investors. Both the equity and debt portions are fixed through the commitment from MUSD to match the GCF contribution. If the GCF does not contribute, the project will not proceed.

This project includes the 500 MUSD loan of GCF as the senior government debt layer by 2038 instead of the LRT debt. The local government debt layer is fixed at 10% of the total investment with a comparable pattern. The remaining debt layer is fixed at 40%, and the equity contribution from the GCF greatly reduces the amount required from the private sector.

## GCF Finance Instruments

The GCF will provide up to 500 MUSD in the form of a grant, loan, and/or NAT instruments such as credit lines. It is not generated a direct income. Improved implementation of the project may generate additional benefits for the country. The GCF grant greatly reduces the amount required from the private sector.

Local governments and NGO investments such as credit lines are not generated a direct income. Improved implementation of the project may generate additional benefits for the country. The GCF grant greatly reduces the amount required from the private sector.

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a unique financing vehicle for climate change action. The GCF was established within the framework of the UNFCCC as an operating entity of the Financial Mechanism to assist developing countries in their efforts to address the causes and effects of climate change.

The GCF is governed by 24 Board members drawn from various regions with equal representation of developed and developing countries. The Board provides strategic direction, makes decisions on funding proposals, approves operational policies and guidelines as well as ensuring transparency and accountability.

### GGF Account Value

The GCF has a total budget of $10 billion. The GCF grant component currently stands at $3 billion and is expected to be more than double in the next financial cycle. The GCF grant component will support a wide range of activities, including climate change mitigation and adaptation projects as well as technical assistance and capacity building.

### Funding activities

The GCF also has the Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding agency connected with external financing through an agreement with the World Bank. The GCF is expected to provide $10 billion in grants and low-interest loans over the next five years. The GCF will be used to support projects that are consistent with the goals of the Paris Agreement.

The contract could be for 30 years (nominally as a 5-year construction and 25-year operation) after which it would be renewed. The proposal is based on a public-private partnership model where the private sector finances, builds, operates, and maintains the project after acquiring the land from the government. During the construction period, the subsidies of the Government are required to cover the cost of the land acquisition and the initial capital investment. The concession has terminated various parts of the ULF that deal with the use of nuclear power plants for 30 years.

If the public transport system will require a 35-year subsidy or not is an open question. However, a 30-year subsidy is of fully consideration to the transport system which covers roughly 80% of the total cost of the project. The remaining 20% of the cost would be covered by the private sector. The public-private partnership way of revenue paying back to the government financial subsidies (including or excluding) is a matter of debate and depends on the political, social, economic, and legal aspects. Ideally, both from an economic perspective the URT is not required.

The NTF will not be directly paid by the private sector but indirectly through the government. The government will pay for the construction and operation costs and the private sector will pay back to the government through the revenue generated by the transport system. This will be a long-term investment that requires short-term financing and long-term financing. The government will provide the initial capital investment and the private sector will provide the operating capital.

The concession period remains at 30 years even if construction takes more time

## EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

### 1. Impact intended (net, SEW, with appropriate 1 digit)

The project includes GCF emission through the LRT and the NMT and last-mile connectivity activities.

**Figure 6: GHG Emissions WTP per job at Transport Modes in SAM 2019 [gCO2e/jkm]**

| Mode                | WTP (gCO2e/jkm) |
|---------------------|-----------------|
| LRT                 | 35              |
| Bus                 | 70              |
| Car                 | 80              |
| Motorcycle          | 140             |

**Note:** WTP is based on vehicle plus passenger emissions. Source: Global Consulting, 2020. A emission factor based on related data.

The methodological approach to determine LRT impact reduction is based on an approved Carbon Management Plan (CMP) for the project. The CMP includes a baseline scenario and a future scenario with the project implemented. The baseline (business as usual) scenario assumes that all trips are carried out by car. The future scenario assumes that 10% of these trips are replaced by LRT, reducing emissions by 35 gCO2e per job per km. This is achieved through the replacement of car trips with LRT trips.

The project will achieve a reduction in GHG emissions of approximately 48,960 tCO2e over the project lifetime (20 years). The project will also contribute to climate change mitigation by reducing GHG emissions from transport activities and improving air quality. The project will also contribute to sustainable development goals related to health, energy efficiency, and urban mobility.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 1 OF 8

## Executive Summary

The proposed project aims to estimate the GHG impact of RMGC-ICO2 using the IPCC-GHG protocol for the climate fund (LCF) program, which is a new approach in Indonesia.

### The GHG Indicator

This indicator will be used to calculate the GHG emissions. The expected results will help governments and local communities to reduce their GHG emissions by 30% by 2030. This project will also help to increase the capacity of local governments to implement climate change mitigation actions.

### NMF Component

The methodological approach to determine the GHG impact is based on a GEI approach (GEF, 2016) based on the use of IPCC guidelines to calculate the GHG emissions. The project will be implemented in two phases: (i) UNDP and (ii) LCFC. The first phase will include a baseline study using the IPCC-GHG protocol for the climate fund program. The second phase will include the implementation of the project.

### Landscape Connectivity Component

This component aims to restore 100 hectares of degraded land through a landscape approach that includes both behavioral change and legal measures. In the short term, this includes reducing land use changes due to deforestation and peatland degradation, which can lead to public support and NMT. The long-term goal is to increase the capacity of local communities to manage their own lands and forests. This component will be implemented by the project team in collaboration with local governments and community groups. The project will also include a baseline study using the IPCC-GHG protocol for the climate fund program.

### Summary Mitigation Impact

The project has a cumulative annual GHG reduction of 17,248 tCO2e and a long-term impact of reducing emissions by 30% by 2030. The project will be implemented in two phases: (i) UNDP and (ii) LCFC. The first phase will include a baseline study using the IPCC-GHG protocol for the climate fund program. The second phase will include the implementation of the project.

This program will help to build local government capacity, including a climate-resilient LCF-NMT and land-use planning approach that can be replicated in other regions. The project will also help to increase public support for climate change mitigation efforts. The project is expected to be completed by 2030.

## Sector & Benefits

The project will benefit the GAM with 3.7 million households through access to safe, reliable, and affordable public transport in the Greater Metropolitan Area (GMA). The World Health Organization estimates that an equivalent of 1,200 lives are lost annually due to air pollution in Costa Rica. This project will reduce emissions by 95% compared to diesel buses, which is a significant improvement for the environment. The project will also contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gases and improve air quality, benefiting the health of residents.

## Economic Co-benefits

Favorable economic benefits have been identified in the project:

- Time savings valued at $34.5 M USD (ICOM, 2020).
- Savings opening and closing estimated at $20 M USD (ICOM, 2020).
- Savings on maintenance costs estimated at $18 M USD (ICOM, 2020).
- Savings on fuel costs estimated at $3.5 M USD (ICOM, 2020).

Overall, the project is expected to generate 1,200 temporary jobs during construction and 1,400 direct jobs.

The project will save 3.8 million euros, benefiting the economy with an EBIT of 26% (ICOM, 2020).

A gender analysis is a gender equity plan that has been developed for the project (Gender Consulting, 2020). The intervention areas include:

- Increasing security and safety in public transport.
- Ensuring equal access to services for all groups of society.

The project will be implemented with an emphasis on the needs of women, children, and vulnerable populations. This includes ensuring that the infrastructure is safe and accessible to all users.

The project will reduce CO2 emissions by 10,563 t CO2 eq/year. This analysis of temporary environmental measures shows a significant impact on the environment. The project will also contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gases and improve air quality in the Greater Metropolitan Area (GMA). The project will be implemented with an emphasis on the needs of women, children, and vulnerable populations.

The project will have a positive social impact by improving access to public transport for 3.7 million households in the GMA. This includes ensuring that the infrastructure is safe and accessible to all users. The project will also contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gases and improve air quality in the Greater Metropolitan Area (GMA). The project will be implemented with an emphasis on the needs of women, children, and vulnerable populations.

## Financial Impact

The project will have a positive financial impact by generating 1,200 temporary jobs during construction and 1,400 direct jobs. The project will also save 3.8 million euros, benefiting the economy with an EBIT of 26% (ICOM, 2020).

## Implementation Plan

The project is expected to be implemented over a period of 5 years, with the following milestones:

- Year 2: Construction begins.
- Year 3: Initial operation.
- Years 4-5: Full operation.

## Conclusion

The project will have a significant impact on the environment by reducing CO2 emissions by 10,563 t CO2 eq/year. The project will also contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gases and improve air quality in the Greater Metropolitan Area (GMA). The project will be implemented with an emphasis on the needs of women, children, and vulnerable populations.

Can it be said that the rise of county councils that would like to hyper-ambitious plans and could educate the public about climate change? The answer is yes. In 2019, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) approved $36 million for a project in Costa Rica to help the country's indigenous communities adapt to climate change. This was one of the first projects funded by the GCF in Latin America and the Caribbean region. The project aims to support the development of community-based adaptation plans and build the capacity of local governments to implement them.

In 2018, after many years of discussion and debate, the Costa Rican government passed a law establishing a national climate change policy. This law sets out a long-term vision for the country's future in the face of climate change, with an emphasis on sustainable development, renewable energy, and climate resilience. The law also establishes a framework for the implementation of the Paris Agreement and other international commitments.

The project is being implemented by the Costa Rican government through its Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINAE). The project aims to support the development of community-based adaptation plans in five indigenous territories in the country's southern region. The project will also provide training and technical assistance to help these communities implement their plans.

# Engagement with Civil Society Organizations and Other Relevant Stakeholders

Since the planning stage, the Government of Costa Rica has sought close links with the direct beneficiaries that are part of the financing proposal. This has been achieved through a participatory process in which the National Coordination for Environmental Cooperation works hand-in-hand with civil society organizations to promote planning, implementation, and monitoring of the project.

During the construction phase, a continuous engagement will be sought. ICF-CER is reviewing today ways to design and deliver the CAMPE program and implement joint activities with community leaders from the action area. This will ensure that the project continues to meet the needs of local communities and other stakeholders.

## S.B. 100 and EIIT

The LIFT component is designed as a DOF. SIB is a finance facility through private sector capital mobilization for the Government of Costa Rica as a substitute for spending. DOF financing assumes the project eligible costs are paid by the private sector, and the government repays the private sector from its budget.

For the construction phase, the LIFT financially profits from a subsidy rate of 6% which will be required to be repaid in the future. The subsidy rate is based on the difference between the cost of capital for the project and the cost of capital for the government. The subsidy rate is calculated against a weighted average analysis by the Government of Costa Rica (GCR) loan portfolio, and the expected cost of funds for the project.

| Table 5: Sensitivity of EIIT to Parameter Changes | Baseline |
|--------------------------------------------------|---------|
| 10% increase in CAMPE costs                      | -2.3    |
| 50% increase in SIB rate                         | +4.6    |
| 50% reduction of the subsidy rate                 | -9.8    |

The EIIT is calculated as a weighted average of the EIITs for each component.

### Next Steps

Continuous engagement and capacity building activities are planned by the DOF. This is justified as an important part of the project implementation plan. The next steps include:

- Finalizing the CAMPE program
- Finalizing the SIB rate
- Finalizing the subsidy rate

The EIIT for the life of the project will be calculated annually.

## Summary on LIF and TIF (MFF)

The MFF and common measures in their own are justified with their stand-alone OBA.

### Total capital investment is 1,673.5 million USD with a requested GCF knowledge of 21.5 MUSD and a GHD reduction of 7.82 MCO2, resulting in an abatement of 80,000 tCO2e. GHD marginal abatement cost curve (MACC) is 93 USD/tCO2e. The project is primarily financed by the GCF impact but to enable a scal, affordable, efficient, convenient and sustainable public transport system.

### The co-financing rate of the GCF is 5.1% of the total investment is co-financed. This is a very high rate.

### Technology-wise the project is based in a state-of-the-art LIF. The currently used diesel fuel system is completely replaced and upgraded with modern hybrid LIF systems. Modern approaches of other development are applied to the project. The project is designed to be scalable and replicable. It is also in accordance with the GCF funding MFF as well as other commonly recognized standards and will have been included in the investment plan.

## E.7 Monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) activities

In accordance with the Paris Agreement, a comprehensive approach to MRV will be undertaken. This includes:

### E.7.1 Ex-ante MRV
- **E.7.1.1** Conduct GHG emissions inventory.
- **E.7.1.2** Develop mitigation measures.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 4 OF 8

In order to meet the GCF requirements, the methodological approach to determine the impacts and the monitoring baseline on a GEF methodology for cycling ways:

1. Impact of the baseline commodity, composed on GHG and total air pollutants. The monitoring methodology is based on the GHG Protocol and the IPCC guidelines. The baseline will be determined using the same methodology developed by GIZ in the project "Promotion of Bicycle Traffic". Reports on the impacts of the LNT, NMT and last-mile connectivity activities will be produced and will be accessible to the public through the GCF website. The monitoring indicators include GHG emissions, air pollutants, noise pollution, and energy consumption for a efficient and effective implementation of these components. The monitoring methodologies include REKs, GHG Protocol, IPCC guidelines, and other relevant standards.

The costs of the impact monitoring including the specialized courses and statistical analysis as well as an interactive platform to present the results will be included in the project budget. The methodology does not only allow for the determination of the GHG and total air pollutants developed impact of the project but also allows for a better understanding of the project and direct with a bearing quality information in the NMT and connectivity areas.

2 evaluations are carried out in accordance with Annex 11:
- Evaluation 1: The evaluation is conducted by an independent Consultant by the company contracted for this purpose. It is based on the development of the baseline components of the LNT, NMT and last-mile connectivity activities. The evaluation will be conducted at the end of the project.
- Evaluation 2: This is carried out with Verra personnel who are commissioned by the company contracted for the implementation of the project. The evaluation will be conducted in accordance with Annex 11 of the GCF methodology. The impact monitoring approach is detailed in Annex 11 of this proposal and includes the indicators, data collection, and interpretation tools, and bears to the impact of the project.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risks and Management Measures (Item 3.2)

### Risk Factor 1: Loss of Integrity Key

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

**Description:** The loss of integrity key would compromise the security of the data. This is a low risk as the key is currently controlled by the staff in the office. The impact of this would be limited to the need for a new key.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- The integrity key will be stored securely and access will be restricted to only those who require it.
- Regular audits will be conducted to ensure that the key is being used appropriately.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Design Flaws

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

**Description:** The design flaws could compromise the security of the system. This risk is low as the system has been thoroughly tested and any issues have been addressed.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- The system will continue to be monitored for any potential design flaws.
- Regular updates will be made to address any new risks that may arise.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Connection, Buy-Out, Consolidation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Low |

**Description:** The connection, buy-out, or consolidation of the business could result in a loss of control over the system. This risk is high as it is an external factor that cannot be controlled.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- The system will continue to be monitored for any potential issues.
- Regular updates will be made to address any new risks that may arise.

### Selected Risk Factor 4: Loss of Integrity of Rolling Stock

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | High |

**Description:** The loss of integrity of the rolling stock could result in a loss of control over the system. This risk is low as the system has been thoroughly tested and any issues have been addressed.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- The system will continue to be monitored for any potential issues.
- Regular updates will be made to address any new risks that may arise.

## Technology and operational

| Category                | Probability | Impact |

Higher than expected operational costs resulting in lower than expected profitability and less capacity to repay debt. This could result from unexpected labor or supply chain issues, higher than expected cost of capital, or a lack of demand leading to lower than expected quality of emissions reductions.

### Mitigation Measures
- Diversify the portfolio of projects.
- Ensure that the risk management plan is regularly reviewed and updated.
- Consider insurance or contractual arrangements can also be relevant for some of the operational risks.

## Selected Risk Factor B: Exchange Rate Risk

| Category                | Probability | Impact |
|-------------------------|-------------|--------|
| **Low**                 |

### Description
This can be mitigated by the payments to the government being denominated in the currency of the host country, or OPECER or the exchange rate being fixed for a period. This could also be mitigated by hedging against fluctuations in the exchange rate.

### Mitigation Measures
- Currency swaps and forward contracts are commonly used by the contracting authority.
- The currency risk is partially mitigated by the development of local and export markets.

## Selected Risk Factor B: Insufficient Experience Exiting Entity

| Category                | Probability | Impact |
|-------------------------|-------------|--------|
| **Medium**              |

### Description
NOCEFR is a change of operating entity. The new operating entity has no experience in the sector and may not be able to attract and retain staff with relevant experience. This could result in delays in implementation, poor quality control, and a lack of institutional knowledge.

### Mitigation Measures
- NOCEFR is responsible for the successful implementation of the project.
- The new operating entity will have access to technical assistance from the implementing agency.
- The new operating entity will be able to draw on the experience of the existing institution which could still be involved in the implementation and operation of the project.

## Selected Risk Factor 10: Money Lending, Nondisclosure, Fraud and Prohibited Practices Risks

| Category                | Probability | Impact |
|-------------------------|-------------|--------|
| **Low**                 |

### Description
This can be mitigated by ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the relevant laws and regulations. This could include measures such as requiring a bond or insurance to cover any losses that may arise from fraudulent activities.

## GCP POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### 5.1 Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (less than 700 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### Environmental Impacts

A description of potential environmental impacts during the construction and operational phases is made. Major environmental issues include deforestation, loss of biodiversity, soil erosion, and air and water quality impacts, as well as community health risks. The project will be designed to minimize these impacts by adopting best practices, such as avoiding or reducing soil disturbance. Additional mitigation measures are incorporated into the project design.

The preliminary Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) was conducted in accordance with the International Finance Corporation Performance Standards on E&S (IFC PSs). The ESIA was prepared for the project through a consultancy contract awarded to [Consultant Name] in September 2019.

This risk has been assessed by the project team and is considered low. The project will be implemented in accordance with the IFC Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability (IFC PSs). The project will also comply with the Ministry of Health, Human and Social Development (MOHHSD) guidelines for environmental management.

#### Emissions

The project will emit approximately 350 tonnes of CO2 equivalent per year over the life of the project. This is a significant reduction from the baseline emissions of 420 tonnes of CO2 equivalent per year. The project will also reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 10% compared to business as usual. The project will be implemented in accordance with the IFC Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability (IFC PSs).

The project will be designed to minimize GHG emissions through improved energy efficiency, reduced fuel consumption, and increased use of renewable energy sources.

#### Water Use

The project will use approximately 200 cubic meters per day for irrigation and other purposes. The water will be sourced from a local river and will be treated to meet the requirements of the Ministry of Health, Human and Social Development (MOHHSD) guidelines for water quality.

#### Biodiversity

The project will be designed to minimize impacts on biodiversity by avoiding or reducing habitat destruction, maintaining connectivity between habitats, and protecting key species. The project will also implement measures to mitigate any impacts that cannot be avoided, such as creating new habitats or restoring degraded ones.

#### Social Impacts

The project will be designed to minimize social impacts by engaging with local communities, providing training and employment opportunities, and ensuring the project is culturally sensitive. The project will also implement measures to mitigate any impacts that cannot be avoided, such as providing compensation for any land acquired or displaced persons.

#### Health and Safety

The project will be designed to minimize health and safety risks by implementing best practices, such as providing personal protective equipment (PPE), training workers on safe practices, and ensuring the project is compliant with local regulations. The project will also implement measures to mitigate any risks that cannot be avoided, such as providing medical care for injured workers.

#### Gender

The project will be designed to promote gender equality by providing equal opportunities for men and women, ensuring the project is culturally sensitive, and implementing measures to address any gender-specific impacts.

#### Indigenous Peoples

The project will be designed to respect the rights of indigenous peoples by engaging with them in a meaningful way, obtaining their free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC), and implementing measures to address any impacts on their lands or resources.

#### Cultural Heritage

The project will be designed to protect cultural heritage by identifying and avoiding sensitive sites, providing training for workers on cultural heritage protection, and implementing measures to mitigate any impacts that cannot be avoided.

#### Resettlement

The project will be designed to minimize resettlement by engaging with affected communities, providing fair compensation, and ensuring the project is culturally sensitive. The project will also implement measures to address any impacts that cannot be avoided, such as providing alternative housing or livelihoods.

#### Stakeholder Engagement

The project will engage with stakeholders throughout the project lifecycle, from planning and design through implementation and operation. This engagement will include consultations with local communities, government agencies, and other stakeholders to ensure the project is culturally sensitive, meets their needs, and addresses any concerns they may have.

#### Grievance Mechanism

The project will establish a grievance mechanism to address any complaints or concerns raised by affected communities or other stakeholders. The mechanism will be accessible, fair, and transparent, and will provide for timely resolution of any issues.

#### Monitoring and Evaluation

The project will implement a monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system to track progress towards its environmental and social objectives. This system will include indicators to measure performance, regular reporting on progress, and periodic reviews to ensure the project is meeting its goals.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 14 OF 18

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan (see Annex 3 for details)

### Gender Assessment

Cidade Rica has been committed to closing gender gaps for almost half a century. Before the area was first settled, women with disabilities were given priority in housing allocation. In Cidade Rica, nearly 50% of public benefit programs are targeted at women and children. The city has also implemented a number of policies aimed at reducing gender inequality. Over the past decade, 10% of all new hires have been female, and 20% of these have been placed in leadership positions.

The 2019 special survey of vulnerable groups shows that 85% of the women surveyed reported having a job or being employed. The public transportation system also provides high-quality service. The city has also developed programs to support single mothers and families with children, including those who are unemployed or underemployed. Cidade Rica's women empowerment initiatives have been recognized as best practices by several international organizations.

Recent studies of gender inequality have been conducted in Cidade Rica. These studies have identified a number of issues that need to be addressed, including the economic empowerment of women and the promotion of women's leadership. The city has also developed programs to support women's entrepreneurship and to promote women's participation in decision-making processes.

### Gender Action Plan

The gender action plan seeks to incorporate activities that will improve women's participation as leaders and decision-makers within the community. The plan includes a number of initiatives aimed at promoting gender equality, including the promotion of women's leadership, the provision of training and education for women, and the development of programs to support women's entrepreneurship.

The city has also developed a number of initiatives to promote gender equality in the workplace. These include the establishment of a gender equality committee, the provision of training and education for employees, and the development of policies to support women's participation in the workforce. The city has also developed a number of programs to support women's entrepreneurship, including the provision of business training and mentoring.

## Financial Assessment (see Annex 4 for details)

### Financial Assessment

The financial assessment was conducted by an independent firm and included an analysis of the city's budget, revenue, and expenditures. The assessment found that the city has a strong financial position, with a balanced budget and a healthy reserve fund. The city's revenue is generated from a variety of sources, including property taxes, sales taxes, and fees for services.

The financial assessment also found that the city has a number of strengths, including a strong economy, a stable population, and a well-managed budget. However, the assessment also identified a number of challenges, including the need to increase revenue, the need to control spending, and the need to improve the city's financial management.

### Financial Action Plan

The financial action plan seeks to address the challenges identified in the financial assessment. The plan includes a number of initiatives aimed at increasing revenue, controlling spending, and improving the city's financial management. These include the implementation of a new budgeting system, the development of a long-term financial plan, and the establishment of a reserve fund.

The city has also developed a number of programs to support economic development, including the provision of business assistance, the promotion of tourism, and the development of infrastructure. The city has also developed a number of programs to support the arts and culture, including the provision of funding for cultural events and the development of cultural facilities.

# No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s)

## República de Costa Rica
Ministerio de Ambiente y Energía
Departamento de Cambio Climático

San José, Costa Rica (CP 10101), 2021-06-30

To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by the Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) regarding Light Rail Transit for the Metropolitan Area of San José (LRT-SJ)

Dear Madam/Sir,

We refer to the project Light Rail Transit for the Metropolitan Area of San José (LRT-SJ) submitted by the Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) to be considered for funding by the Green Climate Fund (GCF).

The Government of Costa Rica, through the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINAE), has reviewed the project proposal and supports its submission to the GCF. The project is in conformity with the GCF environmental and social safeguards, the project is consistent with national policies and strategies, and the project is aligned with the country’s Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and regulations.

The project will contribute to the following priority areas identified in the country’s NDCs: mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions; adaptation to climate change impacts; and promotion of sustainable development. The Government of Costa Rica acknowledges that this will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

[Signature]

Andrea Meza Murillo
Minister of Environment and Energy
MINAE Costa Rica

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP166

## Proposal name
Light Rail Transit for the Greater Metropolitan Area (GAM)

## Accredited entity
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

## Country(ies)
Costa Rica

## Project/grant/loan size
Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- This project has relatively high economic and social impacts thanks to electrification.
- The project represents a step closer to achieving environmental sustainability goals, as it promotes the use of electric transport modes including buses, metro, and light rail systems.
- The components on non-motorized transport infrastructure are relevant and aligned with long-term behavioral change and complement other measures for sustainable mobility.
- The project promotes high-quality urban design principles such as mixed-use development, compactness, and density. The government capital contribution is a positive signal of the commitment to achieve these goals.

#### Potential Risks
- A commercial feasibility study is required by the government for its annual sustainability plan.
- The project requires a financial closure that includes both technical and financial requirements in order to ensure the viability of the investment required from the private sector.
- The National Assembly must approve the loan with favorable conditions.

### 2. The Board may wish to consider appointing this lending proposal with the areas and conditions listed in the new short and medium-term “List” of proposed conditions and requirements.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

- The project aims to establish a modern, fast, safe, and environmentally sound electric light rail system that will connect the metropolitan area with its main urban centers.
- It is integrated with non-motorized transport (NMT) and transit-oriented development measures

# Green Climate Fund

In the semi-arid areas of São Tomé (Greater Megapotamia Areas of Costa Rica CAMI in Costa Rica), the LIFE system, once established, appears to transform 60 million tonnes annually of CO2 into carbon credits equivalent to USD 1 billion. The system is designed to reduce CO2 emissions by 7.4 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (GtCO2e) during the first year and 3.5 million tonnes in subsequent years.

The LIFE system is based on a combination of technologies that include the use of solar energy, wind power, and biomass. The system also includes a component for the production of biofuels from agricultural waste. The system is designed to be integrated with existing infrastructure and can be implemented at a cost of USD 1 million per hectare.

## Components

### Component 1: Systems of integrated cycle lanes connecting the LRT stations and parking stations with optional lighting and CCTV measures; and optional integration with existing pedestrian/bicycle paths.

### Component 2: Integration of a smart mobility system (connectivity/reconfigurability) in the LRT system at its stations including wireless, pedestrian navigation, multi-modal integration with other transport modes, and optional integration with existing systems. This includes real-time traffic management, incident response, and road safety measures to reduce waiting distances and promote the use of public transport.

### Component 3: The CAM network is less than 20 km of non-integrated cycle lanes. The country can provide a minimum of 10 km of integrated cycle lanes while also taking advantage of the existing system with real-time integration of the smart mobility system. This includes integration with the existing system and the use of the GEF Private Project Facility, the country and the private sector to implement the project.

### Component 4: The implemented design and interconnection with the LRT system, including bike parking at stations, optional integration with other transport modes, and optional integration with other transport modes. This includes integration with the existing system and the use of the GEF Private Project Facility, the country and the private sector to implement the project.

### Component 5: Integration of a communication system on the roadside network possibly using the existing infrastructure. The component adopts a communication solution of 100 Mbps linking IGOs using the existing infrastructure.

### Component 6: Capacity building and gender mainstreaming (total cost USD 1.3 million, GEF cost USD 200,000)

# Transport options

The LRT is the backbone that integrates all types of public transport with MMT and functions as a trigger for a paradigm shift in urban transportation. The LRT could connect to other modes of transport such as BRT, MMT, and buses. This integration will rely on an efficient transfer system instead of private means of transport that consume high levels of energy.

## Needs

The focus is on the initial connectivity and NMT measures also involve the use of collection and distribution points for cargo bikes and cargo tricycles to support last-mile delivery, parking, and charging. It has the potential to be scaled up and replicated in other cities in Costa Rica.

### Sustainable development potential

The LRT is a key component of the new-GRID 15 Green Recovery Plan of Costa Rica. The project will contribute to the achievement of the following SDGs: 3 (good health and well-being), 9 (industry, innovation, and infrastructure), 11 (sustainable cities and communities).

From the perspective of sustainable development, the implementation of LRT combined with NMT will result in a significant reduction in GHG emissions. In addition, the project is expected to contribute to the achievement of the following SDGs: 7 (affordable and clean energy), 9 (industry, innovation, and infrastructure), 11 (sustainable cities and communities). The project will also contribute to the achievement of the following SDG targets: 3.9 (reducing per capita mortality from road traffic accidents), 9.1 (developing quality, reliable, sustainable, and resilient infrastructure), 11.2 (providing access to safe, affordable, accessible, and sustainable transport systems for all).

### Needs of the recipient

Green Bikes is a new start-up that needs regular transport services that can quickly deliver goods to customers in the big cities of the country. The models for the population to access a driverless vehicle are limited by the lack of infrastructure and the high cost of vehicles. Green Bikes has been working with the government to promote the use of cargo bikes and tricycles, and to make the PPP financially viable during the economic period.

### Country oversight

The country oversight potential of this project is assessed as high. Green Bikes has been operating in Costa Rica since 2018. The largest market for the company is in San José, which is controlled by 51 per cent of the population and 73 per cent of the GDP. The government has been working to promote sustainable transport and reduce congestion in the country's main cities. Green Bikes has been operating since 2018 and has a strong presence in the market. The company has been able to expand its operations to other cities in Costa Rica.

# 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

## a.
The efficiency and effectiveness overall of this proposal is considered high.

## b.
The cost per tonne reduced in terms of GCF funding is estimated to be USD over the project lifetime. This compares favourably with other projects funded by the GCF, such as the GEF/UNDP project in India. The project is expected to reduce GHG emissions by 10,000 tonnes CO2e per annum and to implement sustainable management of 3,296 ha of EBR of REDD+ per annum.

## c.
The LFT component is financed with a concessional loan from GCF (USD 2.5 million) and the remaining USD 1.75 million is provided by the borrower. This represents a good value for money ratio of 60:40. The project will be implemented over a period of five years.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

#### Project background
The project involves upgrading the existing single-track, diesel-powered railway line to an electrified dual-track railway system. The project consists of three components, namely (1) its system, (2) train rolling stock, and (3) track infrastructure. The project will be implemented in two phases. Phase 1 includes the construction of the railway line and associated infrastructure. Phase 2 involves the installation of overhead catenary systems to electrify the railway line.

#### Environmental and social risk category
The project has been assigned Category A under the GCF Social and Environmental Safeguards Policy. This means that it may have significant adverse environmental, social or cultural impacts. The project will be subject to a comprehensive environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) process, including an environmental and social management plan (ESMP). The ESIA will identify potential adverse impacts of the project on biodiversity, ecosystems, and communities, as well as any potential risks to human health and safety.

#### Environmental and social safeguards
The project has been designed to minimize its environmental and social impacts. This includes measures such as:

- Conducting an environmental impact assessment (EIA) to identify potential adverse impacts of the project on biodiversity, ecosystems, and communities.
- Implementing a comprehensive environmental management plan (EMMP) to mitigate any identified adverse impacts.
- Conducting a social impact assessment (SIA) to identify potential adverse social impacts of the project on local communities, including displacement of people and disruption of livelihoods.
- Implementing a comprehensive social management plan (SMMP) to mitigate any identified adverse social impacts.

The project will also be subject to ongoing environmental and social monitoring and reporting to ensure that it continues to meet the requirements of the GCF Social and Environmental Safeguards Policy. The project will be subject to regular audits by an independent third-party auditor to ensure compliance with the policy.

### Executive Summary
The project aims to enhance climate resilience through flood risk management. The final financial closure will be achieved once the project has been awarded.

#### Key Components:
- **GCF Funding**: USD 254 million allocated for the implementation of climate-resilient infrastructure.
- **MFF Component**: Focuses on mainstreaming and additional grant of USD 3.3 million to enhance capacity-building measures.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP166

## Proposal name:
East Rail Transit for the Greater Metropolitan Area (EMAG)

## Accredited entity:
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

## Country(ies):
Costa Rica

## Project (programme) size:
Large

### L. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

This is a transport infrastructure project, which will be implemented along the central east-west axis of the country between the San José Metropolitan Area and the Caribbean coast. The project consists of the rehabilitation and upgrading of the existing rail track, double-track, signal system, and other works. The project also includes the acquisition of 12 new locomotives, 50 passenger coaches, and 30 freight wagons. It will be implemented by the Costa Rican Institute of Railways (INCOFER) in two phases: Phase I (2017-2021) and Phase II (2022-2026).

A key objective of this project is to improve the efficiency of the existing rail system. The rehabilitation of the rail track will reduce travel time by 30% and increase the capacity of the rail system from 4 million passengers per year in 2017 to 8 million passengers per year in 2026. This will also allow for the integration of a new freight service, which is expected to generate significant economic benefits.

The project will be implemented by INCOFER and will be based on the rehabilitation of the existing rail track between San José and Limón. The project will also include the acquisition of 12 new locomotives, 50 passenger coaches, and 30 freight wagons. The project is expected to be completed in two phases: Phase I (2017-2021) and Phase II (2022-2026).

In addition, this project will enhance the competitiveness of the country by improving the efficiency of the existing rail system. It is expected that the project will generate significant economic benefits, including increased employment opportunities for local residents and improved access to markets for local businesses.

The project is expected to be completed in two phases: Phase I (2017-2021) and Phase II (2022-2026). The project is expected to generate significant economic benefits, including increased employment opportunities for local residents and improved access to markets for local businesses.

# When the LRT is both bold and constrained

When the LRT is both bold and constrained it is expected that some of the passengers who use the harbour model buses should still be able to walk the one or two blocks from the bus stop to their destination. The LRT and conventional bus services are complementary, not competitive. The goal is to provide a safe, comfortable, convenient and efficient public transit system for all users. The LRT will also help to reduce traffic congestion on city streets. The LRT will be able to carry more passengers per hour than the buses because it operates in its own right-of-way. This allows the LRT to avoid the delays caused by other vehicles when pulling into or out of bus stops, and when pulling over to change direction.

The LRT is also complementary as a high frequency service that has been carefully designed to serve the needs of the community. # Green Climate Fund

In the proposed baseline we established GHG emissions. Where is Well (WtW) is presented on a per passenger basis for the baseline in 2019 to present to Figure 1.

## Figure: GHG emissions WtW per passenger transport modes in GCF, 2019 (baseline)

| Mode | Emissions |
|------|-----------|
| Rail | 35.6      |
| Road | 47.8      |
| Air | 110       |
| Water | 10        |

## Methodology for WtW GHG emissions

Estimates of WtW GHG emissions are based on the number of passengers, the mode shares for each transport mode (DfT National Travel Survey), and emission factors based on detailed data.

Estimates exclude emissions from the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) reporting. The baseline is based on the average annual passenger-kilometers per mode over the period 2015-2019. The baseline excludes domestic flights, as these have been funded by passengers using the LRT in a particular year of the baseline. The baseline also excludes emissions from domestic aviation and shipping.

### (i) Baseline GHG emissions

Baseline estimates are based on the number of passengers, the mode shares for each transport mode (DfT National Travel Survey), and emission factors based on detailed data.

| Mode | Emissions |
|------|-----------|
| Rail | 35.6      |
| Road | 47.8      |
| Air | 110       |
| Water | 10        |

### (ii) Projected LRT emissions

Only direct operating emissions are shown for an average calendar year. The same is applied for the baseline and projected years. Projected values are not statistically significant, but they are consistent with the baseline.

| Mode | Emissions |
|------|-----------|
| Rail | 35.6      |

### (iii) Baseline GHG emissions

Baseline estimates are based on the number of passengers, the mode shares for each transport mode (DfT National Travel Survey), and emission factors based on detailed data.

| Mode | Emissions |
|------|-----------|
| Rail | 35.6      |
