# Green Transport Fund

## Project Title: LRT Extension to Beckenrose (LRT-E)

### Executive Summary
The LRT extension to Beckenrose is a key component of the Green Transport Fund's strategic plan for enhancing public transportation in the region.

#### Objectives
- **Objective 1:** Enhance mobility options (MOb)
- **Objective 2:** Reduce emissions (RE)
- **Objective 3:** Improve safety (SA)

The main objective was for the GTF to enhance mobility and reduce emissions of local pollutants. The project aims to improve public transportation, reduce traffic congestion, and promote a cleaner environment.

### Key Activities
1. **Activity 1: Identify and engage stakeholders**
   - Sub-activity 1.1: Launch tender for consultancy contract
   - Sub-activity 1.2: Award consultancy contract
   - Sub-activity 1.3: Conduct stakeholder analysis

2. **Activity 2: Detailed engineering**
   - Sub-activity 2.1: Detailed engineering
   - Sub-activity 2.2: Tendering and construction of LRT tracks, stations, etc.
   - Sub-activity 2.3: Engineering and design of LRT tracks

3. **Activity 3: Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) for the LRT**
   - Sub-activity 3.1: Conduct EIA
   - Sub-activity 3.2: Obtain approvals and permits

4. **Activity 4: Construction and operation of the LRT**
   - Sub-activity 4.1: Construction of depots
   - Sub-activity 4.2: Purchase of trains
   - Sub-activity 4.3: Payment of compensation to affected landowners
   - Sub-activity 4.4: Operation and maintenance of the LRT

### Funding
The project is funded by the Green Transport Fund, with additional funding from the Ministry of Environment, Family and Public Health.

### Notes
- The project aims to enhance mobility options while reducing emissions.
- Detailed engineering activities include launching tenders for consultancy contracts and conducting stakeholder analyses.
- Environmental Impact Assessments (EIAs) are crucial for obtaining approvals and permits.

# The LRT project

The entire LRT system will absorb carbon dioxide from other existing transport modes, including petrol buses, passenger cars, trucks, and aircraft. It will also reduce emissions from the construction of new infrastructure required to support the LRT system. This project will be delivered by:

- The Government of Costa Rica (GCR)
- The Costa Rican Institute of Electricity (ICE)

## Project description

The LRT system will be delivered by the combination of the LRT and the BRT. The LRT vehicles will travel faster for far and conventional bus stops for 20 km per hour and will be able to transport more passengers than the BRT. The LRT system will be integrated with the existing public transport network, which will allow it to provide connections with the LRT and improve the overall efficiency of the system. The LRT system will also be integrated with the existing road infrastructure, which will allow it to provide connections with other modes of transportation.

The LRT system will be delivered by the combination of the LRT and the BRT. The LRT vehicles will travel faster for far and conventional bus stops for 20 km per hour and will be able to transport more passengers than the BRT. The LRT system will be integrated with the existing public transport network, which will allow it to provide connections with the LRT and improve the overall efficiency of the system. The LRT system will also be integrated with the existing road infrastructure, which will allow it to provide connections with other modes of transportation.

## Project benefits

The project will deliver a range of environmental, social, and economic benefits. These include:

- Reduced greenhouse gas emissions
- Improved air quality
- Reduced noise pollution
- Increased use of public transport
- Improved safety for pedestrians and cyclists
- Economic development through job creation and increased tourism

# Green Investment Fund

## Proposal: Costa Rica LFT Investment

The Costa Rica LFT investment can deliver real results. The development of the proposed LFT and SMT and last-minute changes introduced in the negotiations is hampered by the lack of a clear regulatory framework for the project. The proposed LFT investments require stringent environmental surveys and set the pace for a long-term commitment to sustainable development. The Costa Rica government has committed to developing a global investment strategy that can be integrated into the country's overall economic development plan. This means more support if the project is successful, but it also means more risk if the project fails.

### Creation of an enabling environment/strategy of incentives

The proposed LFT and SMT investments require a clear regulatory framework to ensure their success. The government TAP follows suit to be implemented in stages over time. This will allow for the development of the LFT infrastructure to be completed before the SMT is introduced.

INCOVER Costa Rica leading agency for this project (the greatest), has already identified those IDI international companies, part of which are involved in the development and implementation of the project. The TAP will be implemented by the CAIX Bank and the Ministry of Finance.

# Sustainable-development potential

## Aligned with the Sustainable-Development Goals

### The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) that the project interventions are aligned with are as follows:

#### SDG 1 (no poverty): being in a bad house (housing) and no transport facilities have been identified as some of the challenges facing the community. The project will address these issues by providing housing units and transport facilities to the beneficiaries.

#### SDG 2 (zero hunger): the project will provide food security to the beneficiaries through the establishment of an agricultural farm, which will be managed by the beneficiaries themselves. This will ensure that they have access to food all year round.

#### SDG 3 (good health and well-being): the project will provide medical facilities and healthcare services to the beneficiaries, ensuring that they have access to quality healthcare services. This will improve their overall health and well-being.

#### SDG 4 (quality education): the project will establish a school for the children of the community, providing them with access to quality education. This will ensure that they have the opportunity to acquire the necessary skills and knowledge to improve their lives and contribute to the development of their community.

#### SDG 5 (gender equality): the project will promote gender equality by ensuring that both men and women have equal access to the project's interventions. It will also provide training and capacity building programs for women, empowering them to participate fully in the project's activities.

#### SDG 8 (decent work and economic growth): the project will create job opportunities for the beneficiaries through the establishment of small-scale businesses and enterprises. This will ensure that they have access to decent work and economic growth.

### SDG 9 (industry, innovation, and infrastructure): the sustainable development benefits from the project interventions include improved transport systems, making it easier for people to access services and markets. This will enhance trade and commerce in the area, leading to increased economic activity and job creation.

### SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities): the baseline public transport system in the community is inadequate, with only a few vehicles available to serve the entire population. The project will improve this by providing additional public transport facilities, such as buses and taxis, which will be operated on a regular basis. This will enhance mobility and accessibility for all residents of the area.

### SDG 13 (climate action): a primary goal of this project is to lower GHG emissions through the use of renewable energy sources. The project will establish a solar power plant that will provide clean, renewable energy to the community. This will reduce the community's carbon footprint and contribute to climate change mitigation efforts.

### SDG 15 (life on land): the project will also promote biodiversity conservation by establishing a community garden and a wildlife sanctuary. These initiatives will help to preserve the natural habitat of local flora and fauna, promoting ecosystem resilience and sustainability.

# Benefits of the LIF Project

## Social and health co-benefits

The project will deliver better air quality to the GAM area and progressivity in Costa Rica, as well as a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions. The World Bank estimates that the project will reduce PM2.5 by 10 µg/m³, which represents an estimated reduction of 10 g/year. The LIF project will also deliver better health outcomes for women and children under five years old through improved access to clean drinking water.

## Environmental co-benefits

### a) Air quality co-benefits
The air quality co-benefits of this project will result from reduced environmental emissions, particularly particulate matter (PM). The project will reduce PM2.5 by 10 µg/m³, which represents an estimated reduction of 10 g/year. The LIF project will also deliver better health outcomes for women and children under five years old through improved access to clean drinking water.

# fostering public transport and electrifying the transport sector [given the low carbon footprint of Costa Rica’s grid with its high cost renewable energy sources] are the main strategies to achieve the country's climate change mitigation targets set out in the strategy for the decarbonization of the transport sector of Costa Rica (2018) and the National Decarbonization Plan (NDCP, 2019).

In 2017, Costa Rica reaffirmed its aspiration to become a carbon-neutral country by 2050. The country has been a leader in environmental protection for decades, with the government of Costa Rica (GRC) having declared a national emergency due to climate change (2018). This proposed project is aligned to the country’s strategy.

The Costa Rican electricity system, with over 98% of renewable energy sources, provides an opportunity to decarbonize the transport sector. The electrification of road transport and the use of renewable energy sources will have a significant impact on reducing greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) by 2050. This project is aligned with the country’s strategy.

The model shows that the transport sector has historically been charged twice in terms of taxes: once for the fuel used to power vehicles, and again through the payment of carbon taxes. The proposed project will contribute to reducing GHG emissions from the transport sector by providing a financial incentive to use electric vehicles (EVs). This project is aligned with the country’s strategy.

The LETF for electric buses has been designed to address the issues discussed above. The technical design and the business plan are expected to be submitted to the GRC in 2019, which will include the following: a) the identification of the most appropriate locations for the deployment of charging infrastructure; b) the provision of technical assistance to the private sector to promote the use of electric vehicles (EVs); c) the provision of financial incentives to encourage the adoption of EVs by the GRC and other public institutions; d) the promotion of the use of electric vehicles in the private sector, including the provision of charging infrastructure at workplaces and public places; e) the promotion of the use of electric vehicles in the tourism industry; f) the promotion of the use of electric vehicles in the transport sector.

### Financial Structure

The proposed project is requiring grant and debt financing from GCF and other sources. The total amount of funding required for the project is USD 271.3 million, made up of a grant of USD 153.8 million and a concessional loan of USD 117.5 million. The remaining USD 100 million will be financed from CABE’s own resources, a mixture of equity and debt. The GCF LDC 1:2 ratio allows for concessional loans to be counted as grant equivalent in the calculation of the project’s overall grant element. The total amount required is presented in Table 1.

| International GCF | 153.8                |

# Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## Funding proposal for the Green Climate Fund for the passenger express train in the Greater Metropolitan Area of Costa Rica

![Train Image](train_image_url)

**Customer:** Central American Bank for Economic Integration

**Version:** 01

**Date:** 28/09/2021

**Responsible:** Northern Europe Region

**Contact Person:** Ricardo Montero

**Contact:** ricardo.montero@gcfund.org, +41 76 354 1234, Switzerland

---

This document outlines the gender analysis and action plan for funding a passenger express train in the Greater Metropolitan Area of Costa Rica through the Green Climate Fund. The proposal aims to address gender equality issues related to transportation infrastructure.

### Gender Analysis

#### Objective
To identify and address gender-specific challenges faced by women and men in accessing and utilizing public transportation services in the Greater Metropolitan Area of Costa Rica.

#### Methodology
- Surveys and interviews with local residents.
- Focus groups with key stakeholders, including transport operators and community leaders.
- Review of existing gender-related policies and practices in public transportation.

### Key Findings

1. **Accessibility**
   - Women face safety concerns when using public transportation during late hours.
   - Men are more likely to use public transport for commuting due to longer working hours.

2. **Safety**
   - Incidents of harassment and violence against women are higher in public transport areas.
   - Men often experience theft and robbery on crowded trains.

3. **Economic Empowerment**
   - Women have limited access to economic opportunities due to transportation constraints.
   - Men tend to have more flexible job options because of better transport connectivity.

### Action Plan

#### Short-term Actions
1. Implement safety measures such as CCTV cameras and increased security personnel on trains.
2. Organize awareness campaigns to educate the public about safe transport practices.
3. Establish partnerships with local women's organizations to address gender-specific issues.

#### Long-term Actions
1. Develop gender-responsive transport policies that prioritize women's safety and accessibility.
2. Invest in infrastructure improvements, such as well-lit stations and secure waiting areas.
3. Promote equal employment opportunities within the transport sector for both genders.

### Conclusion

The proposed actions aim to create a more inclusive and safe public transportation system that benefits all residents of the Greater Metropolitan Area of Costa Rica, regardless of gender. By addressing these issues, the project will contribute to broader goals of economic empowerment and social equity.

# 1. Introduction

Costa Rica has made great advances in gender issues over the last five decades. This has responded to some extent to the greater extent of rights and other social and economic advances in the country. The country has recognized a sustained increase in gender equality, which has been reflected in the improvement of women's quality of life.

For example, from 1990 to 2018, the country has increased the per capita income of its inhabitants, almost 7 times, as shown in Figure 1 (World Bank, 2019). Also, the Human Development Index (HDI) (detailed in Figure 2), which measures a country's achievements in three basic dimensions of human development, went from having a medium to high level of development, surpassing the average level at the global level.

![Figure 1: Per Capita Income in Costa Rica (1980-2018)](image-url)

Source: Own elaboration with data from World Bank, 2020

![Figure 2: Human Development Index Costa Rica and the world (1990-2018)](image-url)

Source: Own elaboration with data from HDI 2020

**Page 114**

In addition to social stigma, Costa Rica enjoys political stability, great natural resources and a strong commitment to gender equality. The country has made significant progress in reducing maternal mortality, with a rate of 35 per 100,000 live births in 2008, down from 46 in 2000. This achievement is due to the country's commitment to gender equality and women's empowerment, as well as its strong health system.

However, despite these gains, Costa Rica still faces significant challenges in reducing maternal mortality. The country has a high rate of teenage pregnancy, with nearly one in five pregnancies occurring among girls aged 15-19. This is a major challenge for the country, as teenage mothers are more likely to experience complications during pregnancy and childbirth.

In addition, Costa Rica has a high rate of cesarean deliveries, with nearly half of all births delivered by cesarean section. While this can be beneficial in certain cases, it can also lead to increased morbidity and mortality among women and newborns. The country also has a high rate of maternal obesity, which is associated with an increased risk of complications during pregnancy and childbirth.

Despite these challenges, Costa Rica has made significant progress in reducing maternal mortality. The country has invested heavily in its health system, and has implemented a number of evidence-based interventions to improve maternal health outcomes. These include antenatal care, skilled birth attendance, and postnatal care. In addition, the country has implemented a number of policies to support women's empowerment and gender equality.

However, despite these gains, Costa Rica still faces significant challenges in reducing maternal mortality. The country must continue to invest in its health system, and must also address the social and economic factors that contribute to maternal mortality. These include poverty, lack of education, and limited access to reproductive health services.

In addition, Costa Rica must also address the issue of teenage pregnancy. The country has implemented a number of programs to prevent teenage pregnancy, but these have had limited success. The country must continue to invest in these programs, and must also address the underlying factors that contribute to teenage pregnancy, such as poverty, lack of education, and limited access to reproductive health services.

Finally, Costa Rica must also address the issue of maternal obesity. The country has implemented a number of programs to prevent and treat obesity, but these have had limited success. The country must continue to invest in these programs, and must also address the underlying factors that contribute to obesity, such as poverty, lack of education, and limited access to healthy food.

In conclusion, Costa Rica has made significant progress in reducing maternal mortality, but still faces significant challenges. The country must continue to invest in its health system, and must also address the social and economic factors that contribute to maternal mortality. These include poverty, lack of education, and limited access to reproductive health services.

## 2. Background

Costa Rica has been committed to closing gender gaps for almost half a century for more than 43 years, the Costa Rican government has generated structural actions that promote greater equality. For example, in 1975, the National Women's Institute (INAMU) was created as an autonomous and decentralized institution of the State. In 1980, the National Policy for Effective Equality between Men and Women was created.

Costa Rica has consolidated regulatory Framework to empower women and close gender gaps. One of the most recent advances is the creation and implementation of the National Policy for Effective Equality between Men and Women (PNEE) in 2013. The PNEE seeks to promote inter-institutional coordination at all levels, including the public and private sectors, to implement actions that benefit women and education. The PNEE focuses on four strategic axes: prevention and elimination of violence against women; promotion of equal opportunities for women in multiple sectors; securing and exercising women's rights; and the promotion of gender equality.

The PNEE is implemented through a Ministry of Women. It is responsible for coordinating with other ministries and institutions to work towards gender equality. The PNEE 2013-2025 established strategic areas to ensure the implementation of the policy.

- **Constitution**: The Constitution is the supreme law of the country. The existence of the PNEE is based on the principles of equality, non-discrimination, and equal opportunities for all citizens.
- **Party Control**: A High Commission is formed. It includes the participation of authorities of the Legislative Assembly, the Executive Branch, the Judicial Branch, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Judicial Branch, the Supreme Electoral Tribunal, and the Ombudsman. This institution seeks to ensure that the PNEE is implemented in all sectors.
- **National Women's Institute (INAMU)**: The INAMU has been responsible for implementing actions that are limited to the implementation of the PNEE. INAMU and the representative body hold regular meetings to monitor progress.

- **Local Level**: Local governments throughout the Development Regional Councils will be in charge of implementing the PNEE.

## 3. Economic Participation of Women in Costa Rica

32.7% of women in Costa Rica have no income of their own, compared to 15.9% of men. Women also have greater concentrations in rural areas, where 42.8% (31.3% of men) of women have no income. A higher percentage of women than men are employed in the informal sector (60.7% vs. 50.1%), and the country has achieved progressive reductions since the beginning of 2005, where nearly half of the women did not have their own income.

Source: Female Employment in Costa Rica (INEC, 2018)

![Graph](image_url)

Source: Own elaboration with data from CENSTAT 2020

Despite the increase in female labor participation and the fact that it does not imply a sufficient quality of life and is not accompanied by an improvement in their living conditions, it is necessary to note the good household (family) composition of 15 years and over.


---
**Page 119**
---

Without income, women are at a disadvantage due to the loss of their autonomy to access goods and services, which is exacerbated by the lack of economic opportunities for women in rural areas. According to data from ECLAC (CEPAL/STAT, 2020) in Costa Rica, 47.8% of women are couples without their own income, compared to 39.5% of men. In addition, according to the National Institute of Statistics and Census (INEC, 2019), 43.6% of Costa Rican households headed by women have only work in the informal sector, while this percentage is lower for households headed by men.

Figure 2: Percentage of men and women without own income by household type (Costa Rica, 2018).

Source: Own elaboration with data from CEPAL/STAT 2020

The proportion of women without income in the country is slightly higher than the average recorded for the region. According to data from 2019, approximately 47% of women and between 35% and 40% of men are part of households headed by a woman. In Costa Rica, this percentage is even higher: 68.2% of households with children under 18 years old in the country have a female head of household (INEC, 2019). This figure is higher than the average for Latin America (53.7%) and for countries such as Honduras (49.8%), Nicaragua (46.7%), Panama (46.4%), Dominican Republic (32.2%), Chile (30.7%), Uruguay (34.1%) and Venezuela (MC, 2014).

Poorly paid jobs tend to belong to female-headed households. According to INEC data for 2019 (INEC, 2020), approximately 85% of the population is employed in the informal sector, and almost half of these jobs are in rural areas. In Costa Rica, women earn between 34.7% and 60.3% less than men in similar positions. In urban areas, 17% of households headed by a woman have an income below the poverty line, compared to 9.2% for those headed by a man (INEC, 2018). In rural areas, 45.6% of women-headed households are below the poverty line, compared to 30.7% in urban areas (INEC, 2018), yet women earn between 34.7% and 60.3% less than men in similar positions.

In the case of the Central Region, the proportion of female-headed households is higher: 59.3% of these households are headed by a woman (INEC, 2018). In this region, women earn between 34.7% and 60.3% less than men in similar positions.

There are several differences associated with the multidimensionality of poverty and female head of household, which affect their economic autonomy.

When women enter professional careers, they focus on lower productivity sectors or fields of care. According to WE data (ILO, 2019), the majority of women enrolled in technical education are concentrated in specific fields such as health and welfare, which have a low gender wage gap. The gap between men and women is more pronounced in fields such as science, technology, mathematics, and engineering that have traditionally been male-dominated.

There are various factors contributing to this disparity:

- **Gender roles**: In relation to lower-income, women earn an average of 30% less than men.
- **Occupational segregation**: Women are concentrated in lower-paying occupations such as health and welfare.
- **Education and training**: Women are more likely to be enrolled in technical education programs that lead to lower-paying jobs.

The gender wage gap is influenced by several factors, including:

1. **Occupation**: Women tend to work in lower-paying sectors compared to men.
2. **Industry**: Certain industries pay higher wages on average than others.
3. **Education and training**: Higher levels of education and training can lead to higher-paying jobs.

The gender wage gap is also influenced by the type of employment:

- **Full-time vs. part-time work**: Women are more likely to work part-time, which often pays less than full-time work.
- **Self-employment**: Women who are self-employed may earn less than their male counterparts.
- **Industry and occupation**: The gender wage gap varies by industry and occupation.

There is a significant difference in the representation of women in various fields:

- **Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM)**: Women make up 28% of the global workforce in STEM fields.
- **Healthcare**: Women dominate this field, making up approximately 70% of the healthcare workforce.
- **Education**: Women are well-represented in education, making up a significant portion of teachers and professors.

The gender wage gap is influenced by various factors:

1. **Occupation**: Women tend to work in lower-paying occupations compared to men.
2. **Industry**: Certain industries pay higher wages on average than others.
3. **Education and training**: Higher levels of education and training can lead to higher-paying jobs.

The gender wage gap is also influenced by the type of employment:

- **Full-time vs. part-time work**: Women are more likely to work part-time, which often pays less than full-time work.
- **Self-employment**: Women who are self-employed may earn less than their male counterparts.
- **Industry and occupation**: The gender wage gap varies by industry and occupation.

## Notes

According to data from INEC (2020), for almost all occupational sectors, women receive lower income than men. According to INE data, there is a significant gender wage gap in Costa Rica, with women earning on average 17% less than men.

Women's enterprises tend to be less productive and less profitable than those of men. Women entrepreneurs are mainly concentrated in micro and small enterprises in commerce, services, and manufacturing. They are also concentrated in high-production sectors such as STIMI and have lower levels of education compared to their male counterparts. According to the National Survey (World Bank, 2019; Costa Rica in 2018), the percentage of firms with female managers is 43.5%, which is very low compared to other countries.

In Costa Rica, the transport sector, one of the most productive sectors, employs very few women drivers and provides limited opportunities for them. According to the World Bank Development Report (2019), in Latin America and the Caribbean, only 6% of firms are owned by women.

# Transport sector are women, slightly more than the 15% recorded for the Latin American and Caribbean region.

In Cuba, 80% of the workers in the transport sector are women. In the province of Cienfuegos (ICGEV), 2020/2021, 90% are women in recent years, many have recorded a leave of absence from work due to pregnancy and childbirth. This is a problem that affects all types of workers, but it is more frequent among those who work in the transport sector. Only 18% of the drivers of public transport buses and 7% of the drivers of regular car concessions are women (although this does not mean that they are the drivers of technical supervision within ICGEV).

Other sources of information denote the same problem, and again women's access to the labor market is limited. The main causes are: discrimination, violence, sexual harassment, and gender segregation in the workplace. Globally, women are overrepresented in low-paid jobs with little or no social protection. In addition, the health emergency caused by COVID-19 has exacerbated the problem, which 33% of Covid Rioters are seen as high negative impacts on their jobs (50), 2020; but particularly for some sectors where women are more represented in the labor market.

The consequences of the COVID-19 emergency exacerbate women's vulnerability. According to the ILO (2020) in a recent study, women experience a dual vulnerability as they work in sectors most affected by the economic recession brought about by the pandemic (services and trade). They also perform more unpaid care work at home, which increases their vulnerability. Women are more likely to be laid off or have their working hours reduced than men, and they are less likely to participate in social protection programs.

# 4. Advances in Women’s Sexual and Reproductive Health

Costa Rica has historical investments in the health sector that have succeeded in different ways to improve the quality of life through the contributions made to the area of sexual and reproductive health. In particular, the country has managed to reduce maternal mortality rates significantly over time. The maternal mortality rate was 140 deaths per 100,000 live births in 1985; by 2013 it had fallen to 36 deaths per 100,000 live births. This is due to a multidisciplinary and intersectoral approach that has contributed to the reduction of maternal mortality rates in Latin America and the Caribbean.

The country has also made significant progress in reducing infant mortality. In the 1980s, this figure had reached 35 children per 1,000 live births; by 1995 it was around 24 deaths per 1,000 live births, and by 2010 it was 11. Currently, women have an average of 1.77 children.

The maternal mortality rate is one of the lowest in the region, yet inequities persist within the country while the maternal mortality rate in Latin America and the Caribbean is estimated at 74 deaths per 100,000 live births. In Costa Rica, this figure has been reduced to 36 deaths per 100,000 live births since 2008, when there were 59 deaths for every 100,000 live births. The country has a higher proportion of deaths in the type that other countries have.

Costa Rica has contributed to a comprehensive agenda in order to contribute to the reduction of the number of adolescents who become mothers and has managed to reduce the incidence in recent years. In 2008, the adolescent fertility rate was 54 births per 1,000 women aged 15-19; by 2013 this figure had fallen to 36. By 2017, the rate is slightly higher than the national average (15% for girls, respectively).

The country has a significant achievement in the area of sexual and reproductive health with the implementation of the National Program for the Prevention of Adolescent Pregnancy (PNPAP) since 2008. According to the National Survey on Sexuality and Reproductive Health (ENSR-2016) by the year 2015/1995 (15-17), the average age of sexual initiation was 16.

The country has also made significant progress in reducing the number of adolescents who become mothers. In 2008, the adolescent fertility rate was 54 births per 1,000 women aged 15-19; by 2013 this figure had fallen to 36. By 2017, the rate is slightly higher than the national average (15% for girls, respectively).

# 5. Violence Against Women in Costa Rica

The specific data on violence against women is limited and has not been followed up. The last specific survey on this topic, the National Survey on Violence against Women (ENIMV), is from 2001.

In 2001, the ENIMV reported that 37% of the women surveyed reported having been the victim of at least one incident of physical or sexual violence in some point in their lives. The survey also found that 65.8% of the women who had experienced such an event were between the ages of 15 and 49, with a higher percentage among those aged 20 to 39.

The highest rates of violence against women have been reported in the provinces of San José (67.5%), Heredia (65.8%) and Alajuela (65%). The lowest rates were found in Limón (41.7%).

In 2001, the ENIMV also reported that 37% of the women surveyed had experienced physical or sexual violence at some point in their lives. Of these, 65.8% were between the ages of 15 and 49.

The highest rates of violence against women have been reported in the provinces of San José (67.5%), Heredia (65.8%) and Alajuela (65%). The lowest rates were found in Limón (41.7%).

**Page 129**

In the student or work environment, close to 20% of female population reports having suffered some form of sexual harassment. This is a very high figure and indicates that the problem is widespread.

The public transport sector also contains high levels of violence against women. The presence of acts of violence against women in their homes, on public transportation and other services provided by the use of public transportation is a serious issue. These acts are not limited to physical harassment but include verbal abuse and other types of violent behavior while they are commuting. The perpetrators are mostly men who use violence against women. The effects of this type of behavior result in economic, social and psychological damage.

There are indications with regards to the generation of data and statistics that show the magnitude of these acts. These acts are not only limited to physical harassment but also include verbal abuse and other types of violent behavior on public transportation. This is a very little diagnosed information as the majority of cases go unreported due to fear of reprisal.

As in other areas, there is lack of and limited information about women's safety and experience on public transportation. The study "Violence against Women in Public Transportation" (University of Costa Rica, 2013) is concerned that around 65% of women in the country feel some fear of violence when using public transport. This also indicates a lack of confidence in the authorities responsible for creating safe forms and providing that seek to prevent and address this type of violence. The study also highlights the need for more research on the subject, including the implementation of policies aimed at preventing sexual harassment in public transportation.

In 2017, a report by the National Association of Women (AMM) and other organizations highlighted an agreement to address and prevent violence against women on public transport. This agreement was signed by the Ministry of Public Works and Transport, the Ministry of Justice and Peace, the National Police Force, the Ombudsman's Office and the National Women's Institute.

The implementation of the protocol against sexual harassment in workplaces was also highlighted. For example, the General Society of International Cooperation (GIZ) 2018 conducted a survey on the prevalence of sexual harassment in the workplace. The results showed that 47% of women surveyed had experienced some form of sexual harassment at least once during their working life.

The study also highlights the need for more research and data collection to better understand the problem and develop effective policies to address it.

# Data from the judicial system indicate that complaints of violations of the Law on the Commission of Violence against Women (LPVAN) have increased optimistically each year, and are among the 2 most reported cases in the justice sector.

In 2019, complaints of violence reached 35,486 cases, an increase of 7.19% compared to 2018 which recorded 32,932 cases. The data also shows a significant increase in the number of cases handled by the police and prosecutors, with 30,984 complaints handled by the police and 5,602 complaints handled by prosecutors.

The data from the judicial system indicate that complaints of violations of the Law on the Commission of Violence against Women (LPVAN) have increased optimistically each year, and are among the 2 most reported cases in the justice sector.

of the total number of persons who claim to have been victims of rape (13.7% of the total sample), 2.5% for women and 0.8% for men reported having experienced completed rape in the past year. Regarding verbal violence, women report very high percentages of experiencing different expressions of this type of violence: 73.6% have experienced insults or put-downs, 18.2% have been threatened with physical violence, 14.0% have been humiliated in front of others, and 9.5% have been forced to have sexual relations against their will (44.8%).

According to the data on the entry of sexual crimes into the adult criminal prosecutor’s office, the five most frequent types of crimes are: rape, sexual abuse of minors, sexual abuse of incapable persons, sexual abuse against minors and incest, and sexual abuse against adults.

The Judicial Branch and PMU have made an additional effort to keep a detailed count of the femicides registered in the country. The data from the judicial branch indicate that 109 cases were reported in 2018, compared with 76 cases in 2017. This is consistent with the data from the National Institute of Statistics and Geography (INEGI), which reports an increase in femicides from 354 in 2017 to 1,006 in 2018.

The details of sexual crimes against minors or committed by minors are established by Article 194 of the Law on Criminal Responsibility of Minors. This article establishes that any minor who commits a crime against sexual freedom, when he is between 12 and 18 years old, will be subject to the penalties provided for in this law, except when he has been declared insane or when his mental condition is such that he does not have the capacity to understand the nature of his acts.

The data on sexual crimes against minors or committed by minors are established by Article 194 of the Law on Criminal Responsibility of Minors. The data on sexual crimes against minors or committed by minors are established by Article 194 of the Law on Criminal Responsibility of Minors. This article establishes that any minor who commits a crime against sexual freedom, when he is between 12 and 18 years old, will be subject to the penalties provided for in this law, except when he has been declared insane or when his mental condition is such that he does not have the capacity to understand the nature of his acts.

The data on sexual crimes against minors or committed by minors are established by Article 194 of the Law on Criminal Responsibility of Minors. # Figure 11: Registered Femicides by Type (Costa Rica, 2009-2020)

![Graph](image_url)

Source: Data obtained with data from the General Directorate of the Police.

During 2020 (until August 31), there have been 48 femicides of women classified within what has been called "femicide." This classification includes the murder of a woman by her partner or ex-partner, and a number of cases where the relationship is not known. By 2018, the Judicial Branch had registered 39 cases of femicide, which represents an increase of 24% compared to previous years. The majority of these crimes are committed with a firearm; in Costa Rica, in 65% of the cases there was already a history of aggression. In addition, it is important to note that in many cases the victim had previously reported the crime.

Within the group of women most affected by violence, girls and adolescents under 19 years of age should be highlighted. According to data from the Judicial Investigation Agency (OIJ), one out of every five femicides corresponds to this age group. This is a pattern that we observe in the figures related to the statistics on violence against children, adolescents, and women. The National Health Survey conducted by the Ministry of Health with the year 2018 shows that the group of adolescents between 15 and 19 years of age is the one with the highest levels of violence.

Unfortunately, there is no established quantitative or qualitative information on the incidence of violence against women in greater need of protection. However, it is important to point out that this type of violence affects women who are in situations of poverty, women with disabilities, and women of diverse sexual orientation and gender identity and/or expression. All those who are groups affected by the intersection of various types of inequalities.

# 6. Barriers for Women in the Transport Sector

In Costa Rica, the public transportation system has improved aspects of goods and services, and above all it has improved access to jobs, which has a direct impact on women. Data on urban mobility in Costa Rica show that around 50% of public transport users are women. When analyzing differences by age, it can be seen that young people between the ages of 18 and 24 use public transportation more than men; they are responsible for making trips to study or work. However, women are more likely to take more trips on public transport than men because they are responsible for taking care of their families.

According to the National Household Survey (ENH, 2018), women's travel patterns are more complex and heterogeneous; thus, those of them who use public transportation have greater needs. Women are more likely to make longer trips both in time and distance. Women are more willing to combine travel, tasks, and errands into a single trip, which makes their journeys more complex.

In Costa Rica, 48% of the users and service provider drivers are women; however, there is very little sex-disaggregated information on fatalities showing gender-specific gaps in the country for the transport sector for women. When analyzing the number of deaths by gender, it can be seen that men die more frequently than women (60% vs 40%). The data from the National Traffic Safety Commission and the Insurance Institute show that the number of deaths is higher among men (85%) compared to women (15%), and the figures are similar for the number of injuries.

According to the State of the Nation Report 2018, data on the number of trips made by public transport in Costa Rica were estimated at around 3.7 million trips per day, of which approximately 1.9 million are made by women, representing 51% of the total number of trips and 49% by men.

When comparing the data from other countries with international information, based on the ILO (International Labor Organization) standards, it can be seen that in Costa Rica there is a higher percentage of women who use public transport compared to other countries. The ILO standards indicate that in developing countries, the number of trips made by women is around 40% and men around 60%. In developed countries, this figure is reversed; thus, the data from the State of the Nation Report 2018 show that there are more trips for women (51%) than for men (49%).

When comparing the data with other countries in Latin America, it can be seen that Costa Rica has a higher percentage of women who use public transport compared to other countries. For example, in Colombia, 37% of users are women vs. 63% men; in Argentina, 10% vs. 90%; in Honduras, 45% vs. 55%; in Panama, 48% vs. 52%; and in Guatemala, 33% vs. 67%. In Costa Rica, women make up 51% of the users.

When these data are contrasted with the reality of Costa Rican women (IGE, 2018), the results are not conclusive.

- Women generate an average of more trips than men (51.4% vs. 48.6%)
- More than half (53%) of all trips are made by women.
- Only 20% of all trips are generated by women.
- Women have less access to driver's licenses. According to OSYR's historical data (2005) only 36% of the population has a driver's license, and only 17% of these are women.
- Women have less access to private vehicles. Only 28% of women indicate they have a vehicle at home, compared to 49% for men.
- 70% of women and 75% of men use public transportation systems, generating economic and efficiency issues.
- Women are underrepresented in the transport sector. As mentioned, less than one-fifth (17%) of the workforce in the transport sector is female. This percentage is even lower for the Latin American and Caribbean region (9%, 2006).
- The number of women who work on the type or category of jobs is available in the last 10 years; men received a leave of absence almost twice as often as women.
- The majority of household chores are performed by women, and between 70% and 85% of all care and regular care permits are issued by women. In addition, only 42% of staff positions are occupied by women.
- Women have less access to higher education. According to the latest data from the Ministry of Education (2019), 2018, households, the number of male students in higher education is almost twice as high as the number of women. Most of these cases correspond to scholarships, which are granted by the government and private institutions. In addition, women have less stability throughout their years. # Poverty is a determining factor in access to personal transportation vehicles

A key factor in explaining this variation among cantons is the gap or severity of poverty in the households of each canton. As can be seen in Figure 4 and Figure 5, the cantons with the largest poverty gaps are also those that have the highest levels of inequality according to the Gini index. This is consistent with aspects of religion and social factors of poverty; in particular, the case of Alajuela is an example of the above, since it has the highest level of poverty and inequality. It is important to note that the cantons that are considered poor according to the graph are Desamparados, San José and Goicoechea. At first glance, this results in not seeing that there are significant differences between direct and indirect cantons in terms of poverty.

# Figure 1: Cost of households with a car and female occupation (Costa Rica, R11)

## Results

Need of household and mobility factors at the contextual level: in addition to these descriptive statistics, we also include the estimated models for the need of household and household size. The inclusion of household and household size can be useful for understanding the mobility of household members through car or motorcycle ownership.

The estimated models of household vehicle availability are as follows:

- **autobus** = β0 + β1 * female + β2 * occupancy (1)
- **hatchback** = β0 + β1 * female + β2 * occupancy (2)
- **hatchback** = β0 + β1 * female + β3 * family size + β4 * occupancy (3)
- **huggarcombo** = β0 + β1 * female + β5 * occupancy (4)
- **huggarcombo** = β0 + β1 * female + β6 * family size + β7 * occupancy (5)

### Results

While locals or rural households do not display a relationship between household mobility and car ownership, the household who owns a car can influence household mobility.

#### The following settings:

- **autobus**: Households led by a woman
- **hatchback**: Household with a female as the head of household headed by a man
- **huggarcombo** 1: Household with a motorcycle
- **huggarcombo** 2: Household with a motorcycle

Taking an average caution, for each percentage point more households with a woman in the head, it can be expected that there will be about 0.27 more households with a female head. In other words, taking these data into account, if the proportion of households headed by women increases by one percentage point, then the number of households headed by women and men also increases by roughly 1.78 percentage points. This is because the average household size decreases as the proportion of households headed by women increases; thus, the number of new households increases by 0.23 percentage points in a given region.

A correlation exists between poverty rates and the proportion of households with a woman head. The higher the proportion of households with a woman head, the greater the poverty rate tends to be. This is because the average household size decreases as the proportion of households headed by women increases; thus, the number of new households increases by 0.23 percentage points in a given region.

In terms of gender, the difference between male and female employment has a high correlation with the economic status of the family. The higher the proportion of females employed, the greater the poverty rate tends to be. This is because the average household size decreases as the proportion of households headed by women increases; thus, the number of new households increases by 0.23 percentage points in a given region.

The greater the difference between male and female employment, the greater the poverty rate tends to be. The higher the average schooling, the smaller the difference between male and female employment, and the poverty rate tends to be lower.

In other words, what underlies these probability calculations is:

- The greater the difference between male and female employment, the greater the poverty rate tends to be.
- The higher the average schooling, the smaller the difference between male and female employment, and the poverty rate tends to be lower.

The error included is small in natural terms, although the results obtained are such that they indicate the need to take into account the economic status of the family when considering the proportion of households headed by women.

# Project name | Name of the implementing agency | Type of intervention | Gender-specific action or gender impact

## Participation in the decision-making process
### MINAE - Ministry of Environment and Energy (MINAE) | Governmental
Through multiactor dialogue, participation of women is promoted in decision-making processes related to climate change.

### INAMU - National Institute for Women (INAMU) | Governmental
Promotion and care of women's rights through the promotion of Family Violence Laws for women.

## Power affairs addressing gender-based violence and sexual harassment
### MINSA - Ministry of Health (MINSA) | Governmental
Training and education of health officials to prevent, address and eliminate gender-based violence against women.

### INJUVI - National Institute of Women (INJUVI) | Governmental
Training and education of health officials to prevent, address and eliminate gender-based violence against women.

## Preventing and addressing sexual harassment in the workplace
### CANTRAIL - Water and Sewer Institute (CANTRAIL) | Private sector
Through communication languages and training, prevention and elimination of sexual harassment in the workplace and promotion of a safe environment for women.

### UCR - University of Costa Rica (UCR) | Academic
Providing academic resources to prevent sexual harassment against women and promoting gender equality on campus.

## Support for women entrepreneurs
### CCT - Cooperative Credit Union (CCT) | Civil society
Supporting women's initiatives through microcredit programs, providing financial support to women entrepreneurs.

---

The output will need more data on women's participation in the sector, including information on how many women are in decision-making positions, what percentage of women represent as active providers of services or public transportation (such as bus and taxi), and what are the perceptions, as well as cases of violence against them.

Based on the analysis available information, the transport sector in Costa Rica will continue to be a male-dominated sector. However, the promotion of transport services, including the training network for women drivers, has increased the number of women working in this sector. The transport sector is dominated by men and women are not represented in the context of decision-making positions.

Currently, INGENIO is a known as the space where the majority of the activities related to gender equality are promoted. Within the institution's board of directors, 42% of its members include the active participation of the gender perspective and the promotion of equal opportunities for women and men in the workplace. The INGENIO also takes into account the analysis of women's participation within the institute itself.

# Gender Action Plan

The following is the consideration that will be taken into account for the development of the gender action plan. The action plans seek to incorporate activities that will improve women's participation at all levels and in all areas of the institution.

Given the generality of change of the activity cycle to ensure sustainability, and continuity of the project, it is necessary to consider the following:

- **Inclusive** should consider, if necessary, new approaches that align the proposed activities with the ongoing generation's new purposes. Also, it is recommended to identify technical areas where women can be included in the development of the institution.

- The participation of women should be leverage as they recently started their new cycle and reduce actions, which are not aligned with the objectives of the gender action plan.

- The activities must be designed to ensure that the objectives are met. The meeting and the designated team will consider, adapt, and sustain adjustments.

In case an institution is disposed, the activities should be designed to another relevant organization that can carry out the same objectives.

## Dimension 1: Ability and Safety

- Consideration of gender-differentiated ability systems, including intermodality of different modes.
- Provision of violence against women in public spaces and in the service.

## Dimension 2: Representation

- Gain access to information systems, and means of dialogue (representation) a major barrier to women's access to the economic, political and social spheres.
- The new or rehabilitated infrastructure should be designed to take into account the needs of men and women for equal opportunities.

# Dimension 1: Participation

- Promoting direct and indirect jobs for women in the public transport sector, as well as creating spaces for women to participate at all levels of decision-making.
- The CABEI gender specialist will play an important role during the project implementation of the project. This includes ensuring that the actions are carried out with a gender perspective and that the results are being met within the scope of CABEI of supervising INCOPER as implementer of Component 3.

# Gender Action Plan

## 1. Dimension: Mobility and Safety

Violence against women is present in public spaces and on the train does not escape from this reality. In order to avoid costs and economic losses, it is necessary to adopt measures that allow the implementation of a gender approach in the transport system. This will allow them to identify the violence events and provide tools to facilitate the approach of the victims to the specific services in case of an attack. On the other hand, it is necessary to promote awareness campaigns on the issue of sexual harassment and violence against women in public spaces. It is also important to provide information on women's mobility patterns, as well as those related needs, using the train transport system and the stations.

# Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## Table of Contents
1. [Introduction](#introduction)
2. [Gender Analysis](#gender-analysis)
3. [Action Plan](#action-plan)

## Introduction
This document outlines the gender analysis and action plan for our organization. The goal is to ensure equal opportunities and fair treatment regardless of gender.

## Gender Analysis

### Current Status
- **Gender Distribution**: 60% male, 40% female.
- **Leadership Roles**: 75% male, 25% female.
- **Pay Gap**: Average salary for males is $60,000; average salary for females is $50,000.

### Issues Identified
1. **Underrepresentation of Women in Leadership**
   - Fewer women in managerial positions.
   - Lack of mentorship and sponsorship opportunities for women.
2. **Pay Disparity**
   - Significant pay gap between genders.
   - No clear policy on equal pay.
3. **Work-Life Balance**
   - Insufficient flexible work arrangements.
   - Limited parental leave policies.

### Recommendations
1. Implement mentorship programs to support the career growth of women.
2. Develop and enforce a gender-neutral pay policy.
3. Introduce flexible work arrangements and improve parental leave policies.

## Action Plan

### Objective 1: Increase Women in Leadership
- **Action**: Launch a leadership development program for women.
- **Timeline**: Q1 2024 - Q4 2025.
- **Responsible Party**: HR Department.
- **Metrics**: Increase female representation in leadership by 15%.

### Objective 2: Address Pay Disparity
- **Action**: Conduct a salary review and adjust pay scales to ensure gender equality.
- **Timeline**: Q2 2024 - Q3 2024.
- **Responsible Party**: Compensation Committee.
- **Metrics**: Reduce the pay gap by 50%.

### Objective 3: Improve Work-Life Balance
- **Action**: Implement flexible work policies and enhance parental leave benefits.
- **Timeline**: Q1 2024 - Q2 2024.
- **Responsible Party**: HR Department.
- **Metrics**: Increase employee satisfaction with work-life balance by 30%.

### Objective 4: Raise Awareness
- **Action**: Organize workshops and training sessions on gender equality.
- **Timeline**: Ongoing throughout the year.
- **Responsible Party**: Diversity & Inclusion Committee.
- **Metrics**: Achieve a minimum of 80% employee participation.

## Notes
- This plan will be reviewed annually to ensure continuous improvement.
- Regular updates and progress reports will be communicated to all employees.

# Annex B: Stakeholder Meetings

## Meetings leading to the Funding Proposal

In the development of the funding proposal to be submitted to the Green Climate Fund, several meetings were held between October and November in the greater Amsterdam area. The following accounts were written but not circulated.

### Meeting November 14, 2019

The objective of this meeting was to discuss the merits of a multi-stakeholder approach, the possibilities and limitations of information with the open-GFANX model (see [this](https://example.com) link). In this regard, it was identified that the Gender Plan developed for the GFANX model was discussed with different stakeholders.

### Meeting August 18, 2020

The objective of this meeting was to identify the actions carried out by INCORER and the National Women's Institute (INAMI) aimed at greater participation in the implementation of the "Train the Trainers" program. The following were discussed:

- **RECORER** and the National Women's Institute signed an agreement to contribute to the "Education of Women Against Violence and Promotion of Respectful Relationships."
- **RECORER** and the National Women's Institute signed an agreement to contribute to the "Promotion of Sexual Harassment in Public Spaces." Advertising was initiated on posters from universities and externally "Campaign for the Prevention of Sexual Harassment in Public Spaces."

### Meeting September 19, 2020

Since August 2018, the Multi-sectoral Technical Table (Mesa de Técnicos Multisectorial - MTMT) has been cited as a comprehensive and participatory mechanism for the implementation of the Gender Action Plan. The objective is to promote the participation of different sectors in the development of the project and its actions, as well as the accountability mechanisms generated by the different actors involved. The MTMT is considered an essential tool for the monitoring and evaluation of the Gender Action Plan. The MTMT includes:

- **Other participants:** the Office of the Public Prosecutor, National Institute of Housing and Urban Development (INAVI), Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MANA), Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MINEX), Ministry of Health (MINSA), Ministry of Women and Vulnerable Populations (MIMP), Ministry of Culture (MINCULT), Ministry of Public Works (MOP), the Chamber of Commerce, the Ombudsman's Office, the National Police, the National Civil Protection System (SINAGER), the National Institute of Statistics and Informatics (INEI), the Ministry of Justice and Human Rights (MINJUSDH).

On October 19, 2020, during the MTMT meeting, the progress of the project funding proposal was presented, including the evaluation of the results of gender diagnostics and the proposed Gender Action Plan. More than 50 participants attended the meeting, representing different sectors through various platforms.
